 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 51 
   
  
53 https://www.climatefinancelab.org/ideas/climate- smart -shrimp -fund/   
54 For more information on markets of certified shrimp, see  FS section 4.6.1.3 and  From Company Commitments to 
Collective Global Action: What’s Next for our Sustainable Seafood Strategy • The David and Lucile Packard Foundation  Project Component 2: The private sector becomes a transformational agent for change by reducing GHG 
emissions and providing financial support to conserve and restore mangroves that increase climate 
resilience for other coastal populations.  
197. Long- term sustainability is the underlying rationale for this project outcome. By recognizing and internalizing 
the value of mangrove conservation and restoration in private- sector practices and investment decisions, the 
project will lay the foundation for  ongoing action.  
 
198. In the shrimp aquaculture sector, shifting a significant proportion of production area to climate- smart practices 
will lead to on -farm improvements in mangrove conservation and other climate- related practices over 20,000 
ha of aquaculture. By linking these actions by early adopters to public -private dialogue and market engagement, 
the project aims to shift policies, commercial strategies and common practice broadly across the sector, positioning Ecuador ’s shrimp sector internationally and driving demand and therefore further adoption of 
ASC/SSP certification and CSS practices. Once established as common, profitable practices and demand 
grows, any ongoing investment to maintain ASC/SSP certification status and CSS practices is expected to come 
from the companies themselves, either through self -financing made possible by enhanced profitability or from 
private financial institutions  (see activity 2.1.2) . In addition, in parallel with this project, CI is working with partners 
to establish a Climate Smart Shrimp F und
53 that will create a track record of successful CSS loans (as defined 
by loan repayment at commercial rates, farm profitability, and mangrove restoration) to demonstrate 
attractiveness to local financial institutions.  
 199. The CSS Fund is being developed as a blended finance vehicle, with approximately 30% concessional finance 
and 70% commercial finance. The fund's long- term goal is to make the case to local financial institutions that 
they can and should make CSS loans. Although the CSS Fund is not being developed as part of this GCF -
funded project, it will contribute to the sustainability of the work being supported by GCF.  Should the CSS Fund 
be fully capitalized during project implemen tation, this funding will be consider ed parallel financing.  
 
200. Certification schemes are designed to reward responsible farming practices. Globally, buyers in major markets 
are making strong commitments to purchase farmed seafood products from certified sources and ASC is a 
recognized leader in seafood certification. This growing demand provides the incentive needed for shrimp farms to invest in more sustainable practices and to undergo the assessments necessary to obtain certification. The 
project will remove the knowledge barriers that are currently blocking more widespread participation in 
certification and by the end of the project there will be more examples of small - and medium- sized  farms that 
have obtained private financing that will serve as examples for the industry and for the financial institutions that 
invest. The market -based incentives created by certification, and the influence of best practice among early 
adopters, combined  with improved regulation (through the activities of Component 3) should mean that more 
attention is paid to environmental concerns i ncluding ensuring no further clearing of mangrove beyond the 
projec t. Price premiums for certified shrimp can be elusive. Instead, market access via preferential sourcing is 
a common incentive for farms to attain certification
54. The project will support farmers to access these markets.  
 
201. The project will expand a dedicated long- term finance mechanism to assure continuity of resources for the  
Socio Manglar  incentive  payments to communities managing mangroves , and leverage commitments from the 
Government of Ecuador, which will provide US$ 449,000 per year for these incentives  as cofinance . The project 
will contribute to capitalizing the Socio Manglar  subaccount of the existing and highly successful Socio Bosque 
Fund. Four million dollars (US$4  million ) of GCF funding is requested to increase the capital of the Socio 
Manglar  subaccount, which will leverage additional private investment including from companies involved in 
shrimp aquaculture and Ecuador’s PECC . The GCF contribution of $4 million will be held in an interest -bearing 
bank account while funds from other contributors will be held in an investment account and  invested in 
accordance with the investment policy of FIAS.  Projections for the growth of the capitalization and use of the 
subaccount are provided in Appendix 12 of Annex 2 . By clearly demonstrating and documenting emissions 
mitigation benefits , the project will also contribute to creating the necessary conditions for results -based 
payments ( RBPs) for mangrove conservation in the long -term.

  Project Component 3: Create the enabling conditions for sustaining reductions in mangrove deforestation 
and increased mangrove restoration by strengthening governance, climate change adaptation strategies, 
coastal management policies, and legal enforcement.  
202. Sustainability and long- term permanence of improvements to policies and public institutions will be ensured by 
a combination of legal/regulatory instruments and institutional capacity.  
 
203. A key set of outputs of this Component  relate to the inclusive development of policy, planning and legal 
instruments that will govern management, enforcement and government budget allocations. These instruments will extend beyond the end of GCF funding and will include instruments at various l evels:  
• GADs will have codified mangrove conservation and climate considerations in local development plans, 
regulations and policy frameworks ( e.g. municipal ordinances, budgets, PDOTs, COA, integrated coastal 
development plans), ensuring the basis for long- term application of these regulatory instruments guiding 
development and public investment.  
• Harmonized public policy instruments for the management of the mangrove ecosystem ( e.g., procedures 
for assuring transparency in regulation, decision making and enforcement action, inter -agency 
cooperation for establishing technical standards ). 
• Improvements in procedures at MAATE for reception and processing of complaints, enforcement and 
sanctions, reflected in regulatory reforms formally adopted by the Ministry and/or other public entities.  
 
204. In addition, capacity building for national and regional institutions will help ensure that institutions can continue 
to monitor the effectiveness and sustainability of their actions after project completion and improve their effectiveness. Key partners in project implementation have been, and will be, involved in all aspects of project 
design and execution to ensure that they can sustain these activities after the GCF funding concludes.  For 
example, f orest monitorin g data,  specific to mangroves, including robust accounting of soil organic carbon, will 
be generated by the project with the aim of including a robust set of data and methodologies for inclusion by 
Ecuador´s MAATE in the national Forest Reference Emissions Level (FREL). By incorporating this very 
significant carbon pool and sequestration into national monitoring, Ecuador will be able to provide a more comprehensive accounting of emissions and reductions as well as potentially mobilizing additional carbon 
finance.  Also, institutionalization of training programs, curricula and materials developed by the project will 
provide the basis for ongoing replication by MAATE and the Judicial Branch after the project concludes.

  EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA  
This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the 
GCF’s  Initial  Investment Framework .  
D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1  page ) 
 
205. The project will provide both adaptation and mitigation benefits. The direct adaptation benefits come from 
reduced flooding risk of vulnerable people and project activities that are expected to increase people’s 
resilienc e through livelihood benefits associated with better mangrove management. The mitigation 
benefits come from a combination of reduced emissions by reducing mangrove deforestation and from 
carbon sequestration in areas of restored mangroves.  
 
206. Eight priority municipalities , with a population of 3.4 million , were selected based on mangrove cover and 
population at risk of flooding related to climate change (see B.1.1.4).  Flood risk modelling including the 
effect of mangrove cover to reduce risk was used to estimate flood risk beneficiaries and expected reduced 
losses of economic assets  (see Annex 25).  Based on the modelling, the  flood  risk for 8 9,600 people can 
be mitigated by reducing mangrove loss and enhancing mangrove restoration efforts to increase the rate 
of mangrove recovery as compared to baseline rates. The population at risk is likely to increase as 
population grow s, however we conservatively assume benefits for the 89,600 people as modelling 
population growth and m igratio n introduces great uncertainty into the estimations  (See Annex 25 for details 
of Adaptation beneficiaries ).

Mitigation Impacts  
• GHG emissions from gross deforestation under the baseline scenario are projected to be 2,428,986 tCO 2e 
over the project implementation period and 1 1,062,246  tCO 2e over 20 years.  
• Reductions in GHG emissions from reducing deforestation due to project interventions relative to this 
baseline scenario are projected to be 567,404  tCO 2e over the project period and 3,566,685  tCO 2e over 
20 years.  
• In addition, removals of GHG emissions from project reforestation activities are projected to be 164,707 
tCO 2e over the project implementation and 1,042,785  tCO 2e over 20 years.  
• Total net GHG reductions, from avoided deforestation and mangrove restoration are projected to be 
732,111 tCO 2e over the project implementation period and 4,609,470 tCO 2e over 20 years.  
• A detailed description of methods and data is included in Annex 2 2 along with an Excel workbook  with 
the calculations  of GHG emission reductions, including all assumptions and equations used, to enable a 
technical assessment to retrace the calculations.  
• Estimates will be refined through on- the-ground measurements during project implementation.

207. In addition to the project’s contributions towards advancing national climate change adaption and mitigation 
plans, the project will also contribute to the aims of the GMA57, which brings together a global community of 
practitioners to halt mangrove loss, restore lost mangroves and secure mangrove protection. CI is a founding 
member along with other international organizations and the project will contribute in two ways to the GMA’s 
work.  
 
208. The first relates to ongoing knowledge sharing of GMA, especially related to mangrove restoration and “blue 
carbon” information. One of the functions of GMA is as a knowledge hub to share information between 
practitioners engaged in mangrove conservation and restoration. The mangrove restoration approach is 
informed by GMA toolkits and best practice guides. Data collected under activity 3.1.1 will also be shared 
with the GMA as a matter of course (through the Mangrove Restoration Tracker Tool and Global Mangrove 
Watch).  
 
209. Second, the project itself makes an important contribution to the ‘Mangrove Breakthrough’, an initiative 
launched by the GMA and the UN Climate Change high level Champions during the UNFCCC COP27. This 
high-level initiative aims to secure the future of 15 million hectares of mangroves globally (through 
conservation, restoration and securing long- term financing of mangrove protection). This project alone 
represents about 1% of this goal.  
 
 
57 https://www.mangrovealliance.org/

 D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max.  500 words, approximately  1 page)  
210. This project will drive a paradigm shift in the way that Ecuador values and benefits from critical natural 
infrastructure that protect s coastal people and assets from flooding.  By fully valuing the adaptation and 
mitigation benefits of mangrove ecosystems and integrating these into governance and economic 
frameworks across Ecuador’s coastal region, the project presents an innovative approach to the goals of 
reducing GHG emissions  and improving the livelihoods of some of the country's most vulnerable people. 
The current paradigm undervalues both climate risks and the ecosystem services provided by mangroves, 
particularly with regards to climate change adaptation and mitigation. Under the current paradigm, exposure 
to climate risk and GHG  emissions are both augmented by lack of sufficient government, private sector and 
civil society action. This situation is due to lack of adequate information, institutional coordination, 
empowerment of local stakeholders, and mobilization of private sector investment.  
 
211. The combination of bolstering public sector frameworks and capacity, catalyzing public -private partnerships 
and private investment and strengthening local community participation and economic benefits, with a focus 
on the ‘green infrastructure’ that mangroves provide is an innovative and cost -effective approach with strong 
potential for scaling up and replication. Successful approaches will be sustained by local communities and 
government entities over time and the project will create lessons, guidance and tools for application in other 
countries with communities living in coastal areas threatened by climate change.  
 
212. Across all activities, the project will engage in comprehensive monitoring and reporting with the aim of 
strengthening learning and knowledge sharing. The project aims to carry out two comprehensive 
evaluations: at the  project mid- term and at the final evaluation of the project. Each exercise will include the 
identification of lessons learned and their dissemination.  
 
213. Complementing the strong focus on local action, community participation, gender equity and private sector 
engagement, the project will consistently work to integrate these into a strong enabling environment and 
policy framework. This enabling environment i ncludes the strengthening of regulatory and policy frameworks 
at the national and subnational level, as well as creating mechanisms and successful ongoing processes for 
dialogue and collaboration across sectors.  
 
214. The project includes a series of capacity building packages for local communities to strengthen their ability 
to participate in innovative business models, as well as to engage more closely in the land use planning 
process with local government. Building c onsensus among key stakeholders, including government, 
community groups, and private sector actors, is a difficult task, largely neglected because of its high 
transaction costs. However, many experiences confirm that sharing a common vision is a very effic ient and 
effective mechanism through which to achieve social objectives. The project approach is to develop a 
pragmatic mechanism through which consensus can be reached on the operational management of 
mangroves. The project has selected several planning t ools to be formulated through a transparent and 
open participatory process, guided by the entity responsible for their promulgation and facilitated through 
the project. Technical assistance, training, logistical support, and coordination are the responsibi lities of the 
project, along with the facilitation process to allow all stakeholders to express their views, expectations, and 
contributions, and to reach agreements for the benefit of all. It is expected that after several similar 
participatory processes,  community leaders, public officials, and private sector leaders will learn that such 
an approach is valid and worthwhile, despite the additional initial transaction costs, and that reaching 
agreements is an effective means of managing relationships with other stakeholders.  
 
215. The project will strengthen the participation of the private sector through its support to the definition of clear 
land use plans and the adoption of climate- smart practices, especially conservation and restoration of 
mangroves.  
  
216. The project involves the scaling up and consolidation of activities that have successfully been used to 
conserve and restore mangroves  in Ecuador. The area where the project is located lacks the most basic 
land-use planning instruments that could guide the sustainable use of natural resources. These instruments 
will be built jointly between the local communities and the local government as a first step to ensure the 
long-term sustainable development of the provinces involved. The project will facilitate par ticipatory, open 
and transparent planning processes. In addition, the participatory process for land use planning will be 
closely documented and the lessons from this experience will be shared with the national government. 
Legal and regulatory adjustments,  as needed for effective planning, will be recommended through technical 
documents.

*Page 67*
---

D 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 60 
   
 D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
Environmental co -benefits  
217. The project will reduce deforestation, increase restoration and enhance management across Ecuador’s 
entire mangrove area of 156,633 ha .   
 
218. In addition to the mitigation and flood- risk abatement benefits highlighted under GCF’s reporting framework 
(D.1), mangroves provide significant additional ecosystem services including biodiversity conservation and 
habitats for fishery  nursing grounds. Ecuadorian mangroves are home to 179 plant species (Cornejo, 2014) 
and constitute important nesting grounds for numerous bird species. Mangroves are also an important 
habitat for spawning, breeding and nurseries of commercial and non- commercial fish species. The 
structural complexity and ecological conditions of mangroves provide both protection from predators and 
abundant food resources due to the high primary productivity of this ecosystem (Huxham et al., 2004, 
Mirera et al., 2010, Shinnaka et al ., 2007, Wang et al ., 2009). Globally, secondary production of fish is 1-
11.8 t/ha/y (Mirera, Kairo, and Waweru, 2010). Fisheries production dependent on mangroves alone has 
been estimated to be US$100- 21,000/ha/y (Spalding et al , 2010). According to UNEP (2014), the full range 
of ecosystem services are worth an average US$33,000 to US$ 57,000/ha/yr to the national economies of 
developing countries with mangroves ( UNEP, 2014). A more recent review of 250 studies around the world 
that have estimate d the biodiversity and ecosystem service value of one hectare of mangrove reported an 
average value of US$21,100 ha/yr  in 2018 prices (Getzner et al., 2020) . Irrespective of valuation methods 
used, the importance of mangrove fisheries as a food source for local communities is widely recognized.  
 
Social co -benefits including health impacts  
219. In addition to the reduction of flood risks , the project will result in additional co- benefits, especially for 
mangrove- dependent communities and associations:  
• Improved social cohesion and organizational capacity for mangrove management by 60 associations 
will lead to enhanced ability to engage with government stakeholders, resolve internal conflicts and 
develop additional community activities.  
• Improved mangrove management and plans are likely to integrate other social and environmental 
considerations with benefits for human health and wellbeing including water management and waste 
disposal . 
• Improvements in control of illegal activities in mangrove areas, in partnership between community -
based associations and government authorities will also generate collateral benefits in enhancing 
local security.  
Economic co -benefits  
220. Mangrove conservation and restoration is a r elatively low -cost solution for climate change adaptation that 
will generate economic benefits for the government, the private sector, and the communities through the 
avoided cost of the impacts of extreme climate events. The shrimp sector will be able to access new 
markets by producing shrimp more sustainably and bei ng able to brand and market the product 
accordingly, resulting in increased export revenue and employment.  
 
221. Communities will see lower flooding and relocation costs, reduced impacts on infrastructure, housing, 
crops and livelihoods. Livelihood diversification and adaptive fishing techniques will increase the 
resilience, economic stability, and food security of marginalized coastal fishing communities. Mangrove 
ecosystems are the foundation for diversified local economies, with 80% of the human population of the 
mangrove ecosystem dedicated to artisanal fishing, obtaining corvina ( Cynoscion albus ), mullet ( Mugil 
curema), sea catfish ( Arius kesslei ) and grunts ( Anisotremus caesius ), as well as red crab ( Ucides 
occidentalis ) and black clam ( Anadara spp.). Several of these species migrate to the sea and are an 
important contribution to the composition of rich marine fisheries, frequently exploited by the marine fishing 
industry.  
 
Gender -sensitive development impact  
222. The project will contribute directly to increasing the empowerment of women and other vulnerable groups 
in economic activities and advancing their participation and leadership in organizations and enterprises. 
See G.2 and Annex 8 .

 D.4. Needs of recipient  (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
223. Economic growth in Ecuador has directly reduced poverty and inequality levels and increased the size of 
the middle class. Between 2007 and 2018, poverty decreased from 36.7% to 23.2%, while extreme poverty 
decreased from 16.5% to 8.4%. Similarly, inequalit y as measured by the GINI Coefficient has fallen from 
0.55 in 2007 to 0.47 in 2018.  
 
224. However, the economy is still heavily dependent on the oil sector, a vulnerability demonstrated in recent 
years during periods of low  oil prices and the appreciation of the US dollar. The reduction of this 
dependency has been part of the national strategies implemented by the central government.  
 
225. With more than 3,630 km of coastline, approximately 54% of Ecuador's population of 17 million people lives 
in Ecuador's five coastal provinces. These areas are particularly vulnerable to SLR and the associated risks 
of saltwater intrusion and storm surges, flooding and storms.  
 
226. These trends put coastal areas in Ecuador at particular  risk. With 45% of the population below the extreme 
poverty line in the project area, the poverty rate in coastal areas is well above the national average. An 
increasing number of predominantly poor and vulnerable people in the coastal areas live in unsafe housing, 
in part due to rapid urbanization and persistent poverty.  
 
227. Beyond the basic challenges of coastal development, the risks of climate change are placing additional 
stresses on the Government of Ecuador's efforts to achieve sustainable development. Despite the high 
level of exposure and vulnerability of the country's  coasts, there is no integrated plan for the protection of 
mangroves and wetlands to address the combined future impact of sea level rise and intensified storms.  
 
228. The project addresses the needs of the government of Ecuador, coastal communities, and important 
economic sectors such as the shrimp and fisheries sector to address the risks and impacts of climate 
change as described in the National Climate Change Strategy and the National Climate Change Plan.  
 
D.5. Country ownership  (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
 
229. The project is fully aligned with national plans to combat climate change and with a set of national policies 
and strategies.  In addition to climate policies, the project is well aligned with a major new initiative 
announced by the Government of Ecuador in March 2023 to protect its entire coastline and marine 
resources within eight nautical miles of the coast. This initiative is in the early stages of design and 
consultation,  but the objective can’t be achieved without protection and sustainable management of the 
country’s rich mangrove resources, which this GCF project will be helping to achieve.   
 
230. The MAATE is the national environmental authority in charge of designing environmental policies and 
coordinating strategies, programs, and projects for the conservation of ecosystems and the sustainable 
use of natural resources. It proposes and defines the rules for environmental q uality, with development 
based on the conservation and adequate use of biodiversity and natural resources. The GCF Focal Point 
(NDA) is part of the MAATE.  
 
231. MAATE has been involved in the whole process of the development of the proposal, including development 
of the PPF application , public consultations and review of drafts of the funding proposal. Several different 
Under -secretari ats of MAATE have been involved, including C limate Change, Natural Heritage and the 
former Coastal and M arine Resources  (now included within Natural Heritage).  
 
232. The protection of mangroves in the context of climate risk management in coastal areas is included as a 
priority in all current Ecuadorian climate policies, strategies and plans : 
• National Climate Change Strategy 2012- 2025: The proposed project directly addresses seven of the 
eight  national climate change priorities . 
• NDC prioritizes addressing the multi -million -dollar cost of extreme climate events and recognizes 
ecosystem conservation measures as important adaptation and mitigation measures . 
• Mangroves are declared as forest and protective vegetation under Ecuadorian law and are an integral 
part of the country's REDD+ efforts .


---
*Page 69*
---

D 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 62 
   
 • In April 2017, the Organic Environmental Code was published in which there are 10 articles (Art. 99, 
Art. 100, Art. 101, Art. 102, Art. 103, Art. 104, Art. 105, Art. 106, Art. 107, Art. 108) related to the 
conservation of mangroves, among which it is estab lished that mangroves (along with several other 
categories of non- tree vegetation) are part of the National Forest Estate. Conservation of the 
mangrove ecosystem is described according to Art. 99 of Chapter IV, which prohibits its affectation, 
felling, and  change of land use, in accordance with the law, and at the same time strengthens the 
participation of the communities in the care of mangrove ecosystems.  
• The National Adaptation Plan approved i n February 2023 includes mangrove conservation and 
restoration as an adaptation strategy . 
 
233. Ecuador’s NDC does not provide detailed targets but highlights the following relevant mitigation actions 
that the project will contribute to: conservation of natural heritage, strengthening of sustainable forest 
management, strengthening of the restoration of natural heritage, strengthening forest control, and 
strengthening the national system of PAs . The adaptation component of the NDC highlights the importance 
of mangroves and the ecosystem functions that they provide. It identifies increasing the area of  forests and 
coastal ecosystems as a priority action to maintain their ecosystem function in climate change scenarios. 
Further, the implementation of sustainable natural resource use is also highlighted as a priority. Finally, the 
NDC notes as a priority t he ‘creation and strengthening of capacities on climate change and natural heritage 
management in social, academic, research and government sectors .’  
 
234. The implementation plan for the first NDC (2020- 2025) does not cover the proposed project period and an 
implementation plan beyond 2025 has not yet been developed. However, the current 2020- 2025 
implementation plan provides a sense of level of ambition and how the project will contribute to achieving 
NDC goals. The current plan includes a conditional goal of reducing GHG emissions from the Land Use, 
Land- use Change and Forestry (LULUCF) sector of 62,074,310 MtCO2e. The estimated contribution of this 
project  to that goal would be 732,111 MtCO2e, or 1.2 %, over the project implementation period, rising to 
4,609,470 MtCO2e, or 7.4 %, over the project lifespan. Other goals included in the NDC implementation 
plan to which the project would contribute are: i) Update at least three tools for the management of PAs 
with climate change adaptation criteria ( four updated management plans, including adaptation measures, 
are planned under the project); i) Increase the area under conservation by at least 127,500 ha through 
Socio Bosque incentives to contribute to the maintenance of ecosystem functionality in climate change 
scenarios ( 10,000 new hectares are planned under the project in addition to strengthening management at 
60,000 hectares of existing AUSCEMs with incentive payments); and iii) Deliver at least 30 AUSCEM 
agreements in mangrove areas .  
 
235. Additional frameworks, such as the updated National Climate Change Plan,  are still under development. 
However, it is expected that this document will highlight the importance of coastal ecosystems for mitigation 
and adaptation.  
 
 
D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness (max`. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
 
236. The financial structure of the p roject will ensure efficient use of GCF funds by directing that funding at the 
key barriers that are currently hindering progress to reduce mangrove deforestation and by encouraging 
mangrove restoration efforts. The majority of the project funding is directed towards c ommunity -based and 
public sector actions to strengthen mangrove conservation and restoration. The approximately  11% of GCF 
funding used for activities with the private sector  (i.e., Output 2.1)  is aimed at small - and medium- sized 
shrimp producers to provide training,  promote the use of certification standards and to facilitate the access 
to credit that these farmers need to invest in the CSS model and/or achieve certification SSP/ASC 
standards. Investment  on shrimp farms to implement more sustainable production methods will be financed 
by the private sector  and not using GCF funds.  The GCF funds directed towards supporting the private 
sector (~US$3.1 million ) are expected to result in approximately US$5 million investment by the private 
sector in climate -smart  shrimp production methods and certification. Further uptake of these models 
beyond the project implementation period is expected  once these models are broadly recognized by the 
industry to increase profitability and resilience as noted in section B5.  
 
237. The overall efficiency of the Project with regards to expected mitigation benefits is extremely high with a 
projected 4.6 MtCO 2e of GHG emissions reduced and sequestered over the project lifespan , equivalent to


---
*Page 70*
---

D 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 63 
   
 a cost of US$10 per tCO 2e, which is in the typical range for avoided deforestation type mitigation projects. 
In addition, the project will be bringing multiple adaptation benefits to a wide range of people, including 
direct expected livelihood benefits for 41,500 people and reduced risk of flooding for  89,600 people. We 
conservatively assume that the 41,500 people will also be beneficiaries of flood protection and therefore 
use the figure of 89,600 people as the total direct beneficiaries for the p roject  (i.e. we do not sum these two 
figures together to get the overall number of beneficiaries) , which equates to a cost of US $512 per person  
over the six -year implementation period.  
 
238. Grant funding is appropriate for the project because its primary goal s are to secure public goods, in the 
form of the ecosystem services that mangroves provide to achieve mitigation and adaptation benefits . Most 
of the population that will benefit is defined as poor based on Ecuador’s unmet needs index  and the direct 
activities to improve livelihoods are directed at poor communities that can play an essential role in improving 
mangrove management. The part of the p roject targeted at the private sector is designed to enable 
increased private sector investment in climate- smart  practices by addressing barriers related to lack of 
knowledge and targeted support for the development of investment plans needed for unlocking further 
private sector investment.  
 
239. An Economic Analysis for the Project is provided as Annex 3a (model in Excel) and 3b (narrative) and 
demonstrates the important economic value of the p roject . A Financial Analysis has not been included 
because it is less meaningful for this project since its objective is to secure public goods and services rather 
than generate a financial return.  Based on the economic analysis assumptions, which were chosen to be 
conservative, the economic Net Present Value (NPV)  of the p roject over the 20 years  and applying a 6% 
discount rate is US $158 million more than the NPV for the ‘ without project ’ scenario . The estimated 
economic benefits of the project outweigh the costs by a factor of 4.7. Further, the ‘project’ scenario as 
presented in this Funding Proposal provided the most economic value of four scenarios that were 
considered (based on the highest NPV, benefits to cost ratio and economic internal rate of return) . The 
three other alt ernative scenarios included in the economic cost benefit analysis were the ‘without project’ 
scenario, a scenario that only included the project’s proposed mangrove conservation activities to reduce 
deforestation and a scenario that only included the project’s proposed mangrove restoration targets and 
activities.  The high economic value of the proposed project reflects the very high economic value of 
mangroves to the broader economy of Ecuador. Much of the value is driven by the importance of fisheries 
production, which underpins the livelihoods of the coastal fisher families that make up much of the 
vulnerable population of coastal Ecuador.  The sensitivity analysis included in the model shows that the 
economic benefits of the project (and in fact all the scenarios considered) are robust to increases in costs 
and reductions in benefits of +/ - 30%. The model (Annex 3a) can be adjusted to consider the impacts of 
larger changes.

 E.7. Monitoring , reporting and e valuation  arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
 
240. The Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E)  Plan is provided as Annex 11. The plan includes GCF -level Impact and 
Outcome indicators based on the IRMF and Project -level Outcome and Activity indicators, metrics for each 
indicator, methodology for data collection, baseline information, frequency of data collection, and indicative 
resources needed to complete the plan. The Project monitoring and evaluation will be coordinated by a 
dedicated M&E specialist at CI-Ecuador in close coordination with subgrantees  (notably PUCESE) , partners 
and other CI units contributing to the project. Project partners will be responsible for providing timely and 
comprehensive reporting including technical results and financial data as necessary and appropriate. These 
responsibilities will form part of contractual agreements between CI and the project partners and detailed 
monitoring requirements will be established based on their roles. All monitoring data being collected by these 
partners will be aggregated for project level reporting.  
 
241. The Key Project Indicators, including measures of GHG emissions reductions and vulnerability reduction, as 
outlined in sections E2 to E6 will be monitored by the Project’s M&E specialist (in coordination with staff at 
partner organizations  and with the aid of consultants where necessary) applying the methodologies and 
frequencies as outlined in the Monitoring and Evaluation Plan ( Annex 11). The M&E specialist will be assisted 
by the project experts to be hired within CI and other CI units on gender and safeguards issues, blue carbon 
and project impact evaluation.  
 
242. The M&E Plan is a living document , follows the templates specified by GCF , and focuses on measuring the 
project’s delivery of outputs, outcomes and broader paradigm shift. More detail will be added to the M&E Plan 
during the inception stage of the project (the first six  months of implementation) by the M&E specialist  and in 
collaboration with government agencies and local partners. Development of the full M&E Plan will ensure that 
the M&E requirements are fully assimilated and that the plan is owned by the project staff. The full M&E plan 
will build on the information provided in this document but elaborate in more det ail on the roles and 
responsibilities for data collection and management, information flows and reporting systems, finalized 
indicators and means of verification, monitoring protocols and tools, implementation plans and schedules, 
alignments and collaborat ions with existing national M&E systems. The detailed M&E Plan will also include 
participatory methods for data collection and learning and an impact evaluation plan that builds on the summary 
evaluation plan included in Annex 11.  
 
243. The Project Steering Committee will meet annually to review and approve annual budget and workplans, 
discuss implementation issues and identify solutions, and increase coordination and communication between 
key project partners. The NDA will participate in these meeti ngs as a member of the project steering committee.  
 
244. CI-GCF Agency staff, as AE, will continuously monitor the work of the EE through quarterly EE reports and 
meetings to monitor progress towards deliverables. CI-Ecuador, as EE, will prepare an Annual Performance 
Report to monitor project progress and report to the CI -GCF Agency. The report will summarize the annual 
project results and progress.  CI-Ecuador will also provide a final report at the end of the Project . 
 
245. The Project will undergo an independent Interim Evaluation in Year 4, consistent with GCF’s Evaluation Policy . 
The evaluation will determine progress being made towards the achievement of outcomes and outputs and will 
identify course correction if needed. The evaluation will incorporate an assessment of the Project’s 
contributions to a paradigm shift in the enabl ing conditions using a three- point scale rating as specified in the 
IRMF and with reference to the expected changes in enabling conditions noted in section E4 above. 
Recommendations from the Interim Evaluation will be incorporated into the design of the Project to secure 
maximum results and sustainability.  
 
246. An independent Final Evaluation will be conducted at the end of the Project and will be undertaken in 
accordance with the AE’s guidance and consistent with GCF’s Evaluation Policy . The Final Evaluation will 
focus on the delivery of the Project’s results as initially planned (and as corrected after the Interim Evaluation, 
if any such correction took  place). Once again, it will include an assessment of the p roject’s contributions to a 
paradigm shift in the enabling conditions using a three -point scale rating.

---

 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 81 
  
  
 247. In addition to the project specific monitoring of the indicators noted in section E for reporting to GCF, the Project

Government of Ecuador’s reporting to the UNFCCC, the Sustainable Development Goals, targets related to 
the Convention on Biological Diversity and national targets.

  RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT  
F.1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)   
 
Selected Risk Factor 1  A changing climate may impact the feasibility, sustainability or success of the project . 
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  High Low 
Description  
The base line study confirms that Ecuador’s coastal ecosystems are susceptible to climate change impacts. However, 
the impact on the project activities themselves is considered limited. Moreover, it is one of the main aims of the project 
to increase the resilience of the mangrove ecosystem to the impacts of climate change.  The main climate change 
impact s that may have a direct bearing on the project activities a re those that could change t he ability of mangroves to 
survive such as  SLR and physical changes to mangrove estuaries that could make some areas unsuitable for 
mangrove. As noted in section B1, mangroves are dynamic systems and natural changes in mangrove cover can occur 
as mud/substrate moves due to erosion or deposition. However, as also noted in section B1, projected SLR rates are 
below the rates at which mangrove s can adapt to the change by expanding into newly created suitable habitat.    
Mitigation Measure(s)  
Several aspects of the design of the project will address the potential threat that some areas will become less suitable 
for mangroves over time due to climate change. First, t he restoration approach that the project will follow will include 
consideration of the hydrology of restoration sites to consider the current and future suitability of areas for mangrove 
survival and growth. Second, the activities to develop adaptation plans for the PAs are intended to build resilience for 
these areas, and mangroves in particular , by ensuring that potential impacts for each of the PAs are identified and 
adaptation measures are integrated into management plans and current management. Finally, the work on P DOTs with 
subnational governments includes  land- use planning in which potential climate impacts will be identified and used to 
identify appropriate land- use planning adaptations. For example,  this would include identifying areas unsuitable for 
development as they may be needed for mangrove ‘retreat’ under future SLR  scenarios .      
Selected Risk Factor 2  A changing climate may impact the adaptation opportunities provided by the project.  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Medium  Low 
Description  
One climate change impact that could influence the adaptation opportunities provided by the project is SLR, as this 
could influence the area available for reforestation. The base line study included an assessment of the vulnerability of 
the entire project area to rising sea levels. SLR is expected in all parts  of the project area,  but the River Guayas Estuary 
was identified as having the highest risk . 
The vulnerability assessment included in the baseline study included the factor of SLR, particularly  in the development 
of maps prioritizing areas for mangrove reforestation.  In fact, areas with high vulnerability to SLR  (in combination with 
other factors) were prioritized for reforestation. This is because experience with mangrove reforestation projects has 
shown that successful reforestation can lead to increased sediments, allowing the young mangrove trees to form a 
protective barrier against further climate change impacts, such as coastal erosion, and even protecting themselves 
against future SLR (Spa lding et al. 2014; Van Lavieren et al . 2012).  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
The project addresses this risk through Activity 1.1.1 under which stakeholder meetings  will be conducted as part of 
the detailed planning of restoration activities to  record local knowledge on climate change impacts and potential 
future impacts on mangrove reforestation, considering the views of men and women, for use in the final confirmation of sites for reforestation, especially in the Guayas River Estuary.  
Selected Risk Factor 3  There are risks to the project area from potential third parties’ action.


---
*Page 90*
---

 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 83 
   
 Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Medium  Low 
Description  
The project area suffers from pollution from cities and their productive activities, agrochemicals, the proximity of large 
ports and water contamination by ballast water.  These forms of pollution could impact mangrove survival in parts of the 
project area although they are likely to be very localized and involve small areas (with respect to the scale of the overall 
project size).  The potential impact is therefore considered to be low.  These influences are largely outside of the control 
of the project, at the same time, they further emphasize the need to increase the resilience of the mangrove ecosystem.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
The project already addresses these issues:  
• Activities 1.2.1, 2.1.1 and 2.1.2 aim to introduce climate- smart approaches to existing economic activities 
or new bio- enterprises based on climate- smart approaches, which would both lead to less contamination 
from harmful substances currently in use, e.g., in shrimp cultivation.   
• Activity 3.2.1 includes work with local governments, municipalities and prefectures that are responsible for 
territorial management and planning in the context of improved management of contamination . 
Selected Risk Factor 4 Project staff could be at risk due to tensions between certain actors within the project 
area that exist or may worsen  as a result of project implementation.  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Medium  Medium  
Description  
In the project area , there are tensions between shrimp farmers and fishermen, because some do not accept the existing 
rules. The project aims to work especially with local associations, which may lead to a further divide between members 
and non- members. Tensions between stakeholders within the project could disrupt activities if some stakeholders 
decide they do not want to participate.   
Furthermore, there is the risk of criminal gangs operating in remote mangrove areas. Ecuador’s remote coastal areas 
are used to ship cocaine produced in neighboring countries further up the coast to Central America to the United States  
and to Europe.  The risk exists throughout the project area but is more acute in the northern estuaries and some areas 
of the Guayas estuary . Violence between rival criminal gangs  and with law enforcement  is common, making it important 
that project staff and partners operating in the mangroves are not mistaken as being from rival gangs or being 
associated with law enforcement  agencies . 
Despite r ecent gang violence in Guayaqui l, the risk to project staff as a result of  existing tensions is considered to be 
low and in case of actual security issues , CI’s Security and Protection Plan applies to CI staff as well as project partners. 
CI already has a presence in the region, and there have been  no gaps in operations, even with additional security 
precautions that were put into place (i.e. no travel after dark). CI Ecuador benefits from strong and experienced leadership, with a close relationship with the government  and also  with local partners, including communities  where 
this project will work . CI Ecuador has regional and office level Security  Focal Points, which helps to identify and assess 
risks quickly, efficiently, with the safety of CI, partners and beneficiaries  the first priority. CI and partners also have 
strong relationships with the communities, which not only makes it easier to operat e during uncertain times and provides 
CI with early warnings when conditions begin to deteriorate.   
In addition, the project is likely to reduce existing tensions through its planned activities. The established project -specific 
Grievance Redress Mechanism, if used by stakeholders as foreseen, can be used to track where tensions are 
aggravated,  and responses will be identified on a case -by-case  basis to address these tensions.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
For the potential  risk of  tensions between stakeholders: CI  will implement the project -specific Grievance Redress 
Mechanism to track grievances related to tensions between stakeholders and between stakeholders and the project 
team.  The team will i nitiate spaces and procedures for stakeholder dialogue where tensions between stakeholders and 
the project team appear to have intensi fied as a result of the project and provide mediation to ensure improvement over 
time.  The project ESMP also includes the requirement to screen all  the community grant support that will be provided


---
*Page 91*
---

E 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 84 
   
 and consideration of tensions that the grant could create are to be assessed in the screening and mitigation measures 
developed if the risks are considered medium or high level.  
 
For the work with AUSCEM organizations , CI and PUCESE will also be encouraging AUSCEM organizations  to include 
more members to maximize  participation of fishers within the associations and reduce the potential for conflict between 
members and non- members.   
To address safety risks posed by criminal activity , CI’s director for Safety and Security will conduct a thorough security 
assessment and mitigation plan during project start -up. Example mitigation measures include  pre-activity community 
engagement to ensur e local communities understand the project objectives  and that there is no link to government 
efforts to combat criminal gangs ; Hostile Environment Awareness training and guidance on avoiding and reporting 
criminal activity ; and ensuring that it is easy to identify CI staff and partners as contributing to project objectives  (rather 
than criminal or law -enforcement activity) .  
An additional mitigation measure will be an emphasis on selection of consultants and subgrantees who have a 
demonstrated history of operating in the project areas. PUCESE, t he main project  partner  that will lead activities in the 
two northern sites , is well known in the northern areas and familiar with working in those regions  and with local 
communities .  
A Security Plan has been developed for this project and is included as Appendix 11 of the ESMP ( Annex 6).  
Selected Risk Factor 5 The project area is susceptible to earthquakes, subsidence, landslides, erosion, flooding 
or extreme climatic conditions.  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  High Low 
Description  
The project area is sensitive to erosion, flooding or extreme weather conditions, for example in El Niño or La Niña 
years.  These natural extreme events have the potential to disrupt project operations ; however , the impacts on project 
operations are expected to be low since the issues are likely to be relatively short -term by comparison to the length of 
the project.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
The project includes several activities that are mitigation measures themselves, i.e ., they can jointly reduce the overall 
impact of such extreme weather events or help improve the management of their impacts:  
• Local people’s livelihoods will be improved through the development of new bio- enterprises (sub- activity 1.2.1);  
and, 
• The resilience and extent of the mangrove ecosystem will be increased, thus reducing the risk of impacts from 
extreme weather events (sub- activity 1.1.1), specifically coastal flooding.  
• Improve local planning, including identification of areas at risk of flooding and identification of priority natural 
infrast ructure to protect and restore.   
 
Linked to the restoration activities (and related to risk factor 2 above), CI  and PUCESE will c onsult with local 
stakeholders to ensure their knowledge about the influence of extreme weather events is taken into consideration in 
the establishment of bio- enterprises , if relevant  (e.g. regarding the selection of sites to establish mangrove nurseries) , 
as well as in the final confirmation of sites for mangrove reforestation.  
Selected Risk Factor 6  Political instability and changes in government agencies  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Governance  Medium  Low 
Description  
The political stability index has shown a steady improvement over the last decade and was at - 0.02 in 2014. The index 
measures perceptions of the likelihood of Ecuador's government becoming destabilized.


---
*Page 92*
---

E 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 85 
   
 Political risk in Ecuador is characterized by possible changes in government personnel in key positions or changing 
mandates of different ministries because of  elections. One example is that the administration of mangrove concessions 
could be transferred from the M AATE to another Ministry, which could result in changes to concession regulations.  
Since some of the Outcome 3 activities relate to working at the subnational governments, changes at those levels could 
also impact the project operations. The main risk is that c hanges in personnel and/or government structures could 
cause delays in project operations.  Another example is that  a new administration may seek to alter FIAS’s  governing  
structure or rules , which may impact its independence of operations . FIAS has taken several  steps to enhance good 
governance practices, including the expansion of its Board to provide for greater  independence, consistent with best 
practice standards for conservation trust funds . A risk remains that some of these practices could be changed under a 
new administration.  
 
A snap general election for both the executive and legislative branches took place in August 2023.  All the leading 
political parties and candidates embrac ed environmental issues as key pillars of their platform so it seems unlikely that 
there would be backtracking on climate change commitments or support for mangrove conservation and restoration, 
the key focus areas of this proposal. The regulatory frameworks and programs around which the proposed project is 
built (Sustainable Use and Custody Agreements/AUSCEM and the Socio Manglar  program)  are enshrined in the 
constitution and regulatory frameworks and are unlikely to be impacted. Although ministers may change following the 
elections , ministerial level turn -over is not new for Ecuador and CI Ecuador has maintained strong relations at these 
levels throughout numerous previous  administrations and through many different ministers. Local and provincial officials 
were not impacted by the 2023 elections, meaning that CI Ecuador’s strong relations with government at those levels 
remained  in place. Local and provincial elections will happen during the project implementation period but CI Ecuador ’s 
strong relations  with those governments  are expected to provide continuity  for the project.  A new administration has 
been in place in Ecuador since December 2023 and the engagement of the government, local associations  and private 
sector remains .  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
CI has been able to maintain its activities during the last 20 years in the political context of Ecuador and has been able 
to collaborate consistently with successive administrations  and with subnational governments . Elections were held in 
August 2023 and resulted in a presidential change in December 2023, however, not a change in the majority party .  
 
All relevant ministries have been involved in the development of this project, including participati on during Project 
Preparation Funding activities  of Funding Proposal development . The project has been designed to address 
government priorities related to climate change, nature conservation and economic development and enjoys support 
from the ministries involved. MAATE is even provid ing co-financing for the project. In the case of any changes in 
mandate or personnel, CI will continue its extensive engagement with the government to ensure a smooth transition 
and to address possible changes as needed.  
 
The risk that a new administration would  alter FIAS’s governing structure or rules  is considered low risk  and in the due 
diligence there are some measures highlighted to design and implement before the granting process. There are internal 
processes  and policies t hat will be included in the grant to ensure compliance of use of GCF funds . The project budget 
also includes support for capacity building and enforcement support to FIAS.  
 
The risk presented by changing priorities and approaches from new  administrations  warrants monitoring,  although it 
has been reduced since FIAS’s inception due to concerted outreach by FIAS and engagement with the donor 
community , resulting in reforms to FIAS’s governance . It is unlikely that there would be dramatic additional reforms to 
FIAS so soon after the Government of Ecuador helped restructure it in 2017 .   
 
Of note,  the vast majority of F und decisions are made by an Administrative Board (“Junta Administrativa” or “JA”) for 
each of FIAS’s  subsidiary funds  (such as the Socio Bosque Fund) . These JAs were created during financing 
agreements with donors, are largely determined according to the requirements in such agreements, and largely operate 
independent of the FIAS Board of Directors . The governance structure of each of these JAs is defined in their governing 
rules / operations manuals which may be subject to modification based on FIAS and donor agreement s. The JAs are 
primarily respons ible for supporting the ex ecution of projects, making decisions on fund allocations and disbursements, 
consistent with the terms of any donor agreement. The J As are respons ible for the evaluation and oversight of donor


---
*Page 93*
---

E 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 86 
   
 supported projects / funds. Accordingly, CI will ensure  in its grant agreement  with FIAS that the JA managing the  Socio 
Manglar  subaccount meets best practices , including any GCF requirements.  
Selected Risk Factor 7  Prohibited Practices,  including ML/FT  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Prohibited practices  Low Medium  
Description  
The Project involves a wide array of actors, including government, sub -grantees, vendors and service providers, and  
beneficiaries.  Based on a calculation using multiple international risk indicators, CI assesses Ecuador to be a low -risk 
country for GCF prohibited practices, including terrorist financing and money laundering. CI Ecuador  follows and is 
subject to CI‘s Anti- Money Laundering and Counter -Terrorism -Financing (AML/C FT) Policy and related procedures , 
which have been assessed as effective by the GCF. In addition, CI -Ecuador  is subject to national laws related to 
AML/CFT, for which it has a country -specific Manual for Prevention of ML and FT. In compliance with the Organic 
Regulation to the Law of Prevention and Detection of Laundering Money and Financing Terrorism, CI-Ecuador’s 
AML/CFT  practices are supervised by a public entity, the Financial Analysis Unit ( Unidad de Análisis Financiero  
[UAFE ]), which conducts a thorough review of financial procedures related to AML/CFT and to which CI -Ecuador  must 
submit monthly reports. Finally,  the project’s major non -EE recipients of GCF funds, sub- grantees PUCESE  and FIAS,  
were screened and cleared against international sanctions lists .  
 
Concerning impact, serious breaches in prohibited practices, including ML/FT, would impact the reputation of CI and 
the project.   
Mitigation Measure(s)  
CI has several procedures and controls in place to prevent, mitigate, and identify risk. In order to comply with CI 
AML/CTF, U.S. law, and GCF fiduciary standards, CI applies rigorous C FT/AML screening to all CI funding recipients. 
CI also conducts an assessment of Prohibited Practices (PP) risks of grant recipients and includes any required 
mitigation measures in its legally binding grant agreements. All EE staff and subgrantees must also undergo training 
on PP (including red flags and how to report violat ions), and all grant and services agreements (used by both CI and 
sub-grantees) must include language on compliance with the GCF Policy on Prohibited Practices , including AML/CFT . 
Sub-grantees PUCESE and FIAS will receive capacity -building related to AML/CFT and prohibited practices from CI 
Ecuador , and c ompliance with PP policies and mitigation measures is part of project monitoring, including site visits 
and CI audits, throughout implementation.  
To report and remedy risks, any CI employee; employee of an executing entity, grantee, partner, or vendor; or 
beneficiary may report a grievance or violation of these policies anonymously online or by telephone through CI’s 
Ethics Hotline. All reported gri evances will be investigated and addressed per CI’s Anti -Fraud Policy & Guidelines for 
Investigations. Full details can be found on CI’s website at https://www.conservation.org/about/our -policies/reporting-
illegal- or-unethical -conduct -statement . 
Selected Risk Factor 8  Unrealized or delayed co -financing  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Medium  Low 
Description  
Co-financing plays a n important part in achieving the full impact of the proposed project . There is a medium risk of 
delay ed or unrealized co- financing for the project , because MAATE’s grant co- financing depends on the approval  
processes for government budgets. However, t he committed co-financing is proposed for activit y A.1.1.1 and therefore, 
the negative impact to the project would be limited to th is activit y. In addition, a portion of the co -financing committed 
by MAATE is ‘in-kind’ and related to staff salaries  and therefore poses a low risk of not materializing.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
The project team  will periodically assess the degree of risk (both likelihood and impact) that co -financing will be funded 
and its associated impact on project implementation.  CI will also conduct regular engagement with co- financing sources 
to understand and manage this risk.  In the event that co -financing is not realized, CI will try to identify alternative 
sources . If that is not possible , CI would work with GCF  within the framework of the project’s FAA  to propose adaptive

Selected Risk Factor 9  Risk of inflation  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Medium  Low 
Description  
Although currently decreasing, Ecuador’s inflation rate for 2022 was 3.7% according to the International Monetary 
Fund’s Data Mapper (April 2023). Ecuador’s  average inflation rate over the period 2013 to 2022 was 1.8%  with a 
maximum of 4.2% in 201764. This period includes several turbulent years for Ecuador’s economy due to fluctuations in 
the global price of oil, on which Ecuador’s economy is highly reliant . The IMF’s forecast for inflation in Ecuador is 1.34% 
in 2025, risi ng to 1.54% in 2026 through to 202865. However, given the increases in inflation globally, the risk of rising 
inflation is considered a ‘medium ’ risk to this project as it could impact the budget, co- financing and activity 
implementation.  Higher than expected inflation would incr ease costs across the whole project , making it harder to 
achieve all of the project activities . High in flation rates  would be accompanied by high interest rates at financial 
institutions, which could impact the likelihood of private sector shrimp farm companies taking loans to invest in climate 
smart shrimp practices (Activity 2.1.2 ).  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
To mitigate for the impacts of inflation, CI has included inflation considerations into its budget figures. The budget has 
been prepared assuming an inflation rate of 3%.  The 3% figure is above the IMF’s forecast but given the challenges of 
inflation forecasting  and recent global increases in inflation, CI has used the 3% figure out of precaution. In addition, CI 
will regularly monitor inflation pressures on the project budget and continually seek to identify cost savings where 
possible.

  GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS  
G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment  (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)   
248. The Environmental and Social risk screening for the project indicated that a limited and adequate Environmental 
and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) be conducted to identify potential project risks. This assessment resulted 
in the following:  
 249. The project is risk  category B (moderate risk) . 
 250. An Environmental and Social Impact Assessment and Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) , 
included as Annex 6,  includes a summary of the project and the applicable legal and institutional framework, 
the risks identified, the action plan with mitigation measures and implementation modalities. Also included are a 
Grievance Redress Mechanism as well as:  
• Natural Habitat Protection Plan;  
• Process Framework for Restricti on of Access to Natural Resources;  
• Plan for Afro -descendant Communities and Cholos Pescadores;  
• Stakeholder Engagement Plan (Annex 7 ); and  
• Gender Action Plan (Annex 8 ).  
 
251. The project does not intend to cause negative environmental or social impacts; on the contrary, it aims to increase the resilience of the mangrove ecosystem and benefit local communities. It is based on rights -based 
and inclusive approaches, including measures to ensure the participation of vulnerable groups, such as women, 
youth and others.   
 
252. Some  risks are, by their nature, beyond the control of the project. For example, climate change may impact the 
project area and there are localized  impacts on the mangrove ecosystem from pollution in some places. The 
project includes a variety of activities to address these issues.  
 253. Some details of activities will be defined during  the project and therefore risk screening and appropriate 
mitigation measures for these will be identified as part of the project act ivities . Screened activities that result in 
a category B risk rating will require further limited ESIAs and ESMPs to be developed as per the guidance 
provided in Annex 6 . This applies to:  
• Activities 1.2.1  and 1.2.2: It is necessary to identify which community livelihoods activities and small -scale 
enterprises to be supported in various locations  near the time of the work . Once the options and locations for 
their development have been prioritized, their potential impacts must be assessed.  
• Activities 1.1.1: Project preparation included the identification of areas with potential for reforestation. The 
resulting map ( see Annex 2 ) defines a potential of 10,000 ha while the project includes 4,850 ha as a target, so 
the detailed selection of sites for reforestation is pending. To address this , detailed reforestation plans will be 
developed as part of the reforestation activities including assessment of risks and measures to avoid 
environmental damage and negative social impacts . 
 
254. Apart from those mentioned above, the most important risks and their mitigation measures are:

G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)   
255. During Project preparation, a gender assessment (see Annex 8 ) was undertaken to understand the differential 
vulnerability to, and impact of, climate change on women and men in the project area. From the findings of this 
assessment and an analysis of the proposed project design, a Gender Action Plan (GAP) was developed. The 
process to produce these components included a desktop review and analysis of available literature and relevant legislation, policy and programs in the country, as well as key informant interviews, household questionnaires, 
focus group discussions and consultation workshops with project stakeholders and beneficiaries.  
 
256. The GAP  (see Annex 8) identifies opportunities to close gender gaps , reduce gender -based violence (GBV)  and 
the included actions are largely concentrated in the support of mangrove associations where direct collaboration 
with the women and men who depend on, and manage, the mangroves will occur. These include mangrove use 
agreements that integrate gender co nsiderations, tailored and specific support to women in mangrove 
associations (including the two women’s associations), setting targets (50/50) for women’s and men’s benefit 
from the adoption of diversified and climate resilient livelihoods options linked to mangroves, and ensuring that 
project staff and delivery partners are trained on gender equity principles and the importance of mitigating gender inequalities in the project, including gender -based violence.  
 
257. To support implementation of the GAP, CI will employ a full -time Gender Manager to ensure robust gender 
analysis and lead the mainstreaming of gender into the project . The Gender Manager will develop and deliver 
appropriate capacity building for colleagues and delivery partners that is well -designed, clear, tailored to the 
context, and practical. The Gender Manager will accompany project teams to the field to ground truth progress 
on gender outcomes: meet with women/women’s groups, collect qualitative information, help project teams to 
problem solve, propose adaptive changes  and participate in report  preparation. The mana ger will s upport the four 
estuaries  and, in particular , the social coordinat ors in the implementation of the GAP. The manager will also 
collaborate with CI’s global gender program to ensure consistency in gender approach across the institution and will be support ed by CI’s global Gender Advisor. Th e Gender Manager position will be based in Guayaquil city.  
 
258. A complex set of historical, social, cultural, and economic factors influence the project sites’ current gender norms and inequalities . There is a lack of political representation by women in decision- making, time deficit du e to mostly 
reproductive labor impinging on their ability to engage in community, organizational, and project activities, and 
finally high rates of gender -based violence that further highlights inequalities and is an important barrier that


---
*Page 97*
---

F 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 90 
   
 keeps women from exercising their voice and agency in mangrove conservation. This situation is compounded 
by a number of structural problems associated with education, health, the provision of utilities and limited 
employment opportunities.  
 
259. In these mangrove- dependent communities, women are more vulnerable and experience higher rates of poverty 
than men. Men tend to control the main economic activity in the communities: the harvesting and sale of seafood. 
Often it is men who hold formal posit ions of leadership within communities and fishing associations.  
 
260. There are distinct gender differences in mangrove use and management in the northern sites vers us those in the 
south. In the north, women walk (often collectively) to the mangroves and are therefore more actively engaged; 
Mangrove associations in the north also have ~60% women members. The south is more male dominated; 
women’s access and use of the mangrove areas is constrained by distance as the mangroves are far from the 
community and must be accessed by boat (often individually). Because they are not familiar with the mangroves, they are largely absent from the associations; mangrove associations in the south have ~15% women members. 
 
 261. Women living  in mangroves work long days, which include family caregiving responsibilities, harvesting or fishing 
activities, cooking for the family and looking after the children. The average working day for women is around 14 
hours and little societal value is place d on their caregiving or reproductive tasks. By contrast, men have much 
shorter days, fishing or harvesting for between four and five hours. This means that once their day’s fishing or 
harvesting is over, they are relieved from work.  
 262. Rates of GBV are very high (67% in project sites) and could be perpetuated/increased by climate change impacts 
that result in economic disruption.  
 263. As a result of gender norms, men and women are impacted differently by climate related events. Rising sea levels and flooding – the two major effects cited in justifying climate change adaptation action in these project sites – 
can also have consequences like an increase in water salinity and water -borne diseases, which are likely to affect 
primarily women, who tend to be responsible for collecting water for the household and caring for sick family  
members. Furthermore, existing gender inequalities may be exacerbated by the dynamics of climate change in 
the project area.  
 
G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
264. The project will be executed by CI-Ecuador,  and therefore, financial management and procurement for this project 
will follow relevant CI policies and procedures, as well as relevant provisions in the Accreditation Master 
Agreement (AMA) signed between CI and the GCF.  
 
Financial management:  
 
265. CI uses Unit4 Business World software suite as its enterprise resource planning (ERP) system for financial 
accounting and monitoring in all its global operations. This system supports financial monitoring, reporting, human 
resources, procurement, and recor d-keeping in line with the requirements of CI’s AMA. It supports a system of 
ledger accounts to separate and track income and expenditure by donor, project, and subaward.  It also provides 
a coding system for monitoring and reporting of projects. In additi on, all EEs and sub- grantees will be required to 
submit quarterly financial reports that will be systematically reviewed by the AE and EE, respectively.  
 
266. An annual audit of the project will be conducted by an independent external audit firm in accordance with audit 
standards under U.S. GAAP and furnished to the GCF in accordance with CI’s AMA.  
 
267. Disbursements made to CI will be recorded to the ledger account for this GCF project, which can be tracked using 
the Business World system. Disbursements from the EE to sub- grantee will be made upon CI’s receipt of 
acceptable progress and financial reports  and any other requirements as included in the applicable grant 
agreement.  
 
268. CI has procedures and controls in place to prevent risk of prohibited practices, including ML/FT (collectively, ‘PP’). 
CI applies rigorous CTF/AML screening to all CI funding recipients. CI also assesses PP risks of grant recipients 
and includes mitigation measures in its grant agreements. All CI staff and subgrantees must also undergo training 
on PP (including red flags and how to report violations), and all grant and services agreements (used by both CI 
and sub- grantees) must include language on complianc e with the GCF Policy on Prohibited Practices. 
Compliance with PP policies and mitigation measures is part of project monitoring, including site visits and CI

 internal audits, throughout project implementation. Violations of PPs, fraud, or financial misuse may be reported
