Secretariat ’s assessment  of FP 235  
Proposal name:  Mangroves for Climate: Public, Private and Community 
Partnerships for Mitigation and Adaptation in Ecuador  
Accredited entity:  Conservation International Foundation (CI)  
Country:  Ecuador  
Project/programme size:  Small 
I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat  
1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following 
remarks:  
Strengths  Points of caution  
Mangroves model : The project involves  
government, local communities and the 
private sector in supporting community -
based  enterprises and  community mangrove 
management teams undertaking income 
generation while restoring and sustainably 
managing mangroves  based on a results -
based payment scheme . In addition to 
mitigation benefits,  the mangroves  provide 
ecosystem services that protect coastal 
communities and upstream communities 
from salinization caused by  floods and sea 
level rise.   
Community grants : The project is working 
with a trust fund and government finance 
that aims to empower  local communities to 
sustainably manage large areas  of mangroves 
via community management agreements  that 
grant reward payments for their  
management activities.   
Transform ation of the  private sector : The 
project will accelerate the transformation of 
the shrimp industry from being a driver of 
mangrove deforestation to a driver  of 
preservation , while shrimp farmers will be 
able to access a premium price for certified 
shrimp cultivation.


---
*Page 107*
---

 
GCF/B. 39/02/Add.  10 
Page 107 
 
 
Financial sustainability model : The project 
will help to capitalize the trust fund 
subaccount on results -based payments for 
community mangrove management , with  
additional funding to be contributed  annually 
from private and government sources. The 
trust fund is expected to run in perpetuity , 
hence generating  impact beyond project 
implementation and even beyond the 20 -year 
project life span .  
Scalability and replicability:  The project 
may allow Ecuador to become a 
demonstration site for climate -smart shrimp 
farming that can be scaled and replicated 
across different regions, especially due to the 
project ’s link with the Global Mangrove 
Alliance .  
2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the 
term sheet agreed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity, and, if considered 
appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in Annex II of document GCF/B.39/02.  
II. Summary of the Secretariat ’s assessment  
2.1 Project background  
3. The project background , as set out in the funding proposal,  underscores the pressing 
need to address the impacts of climate change on Ecuador ’s coastal regions. Climate change 
projections indicate increasing risks , such a s sea  level  rise, extreme weather events and coastal 
flooding, that threaten local communities and economic activities. Mangroves play a crucial role 
as natural barriers against erosion and flooding, yet ongoing deforestation exacerbates these 
risks. Despite recent conservation efforts, man grove loss continues, with adverse consequences 
for carbon sequestration and biodiversity.  
4. The proposed interventions aim to address these challenges by engaging local 
communities, the private sector and government entities in the  protect ion and restor ation  of the 
mangroves. Through effective mangrove management and restoration, the project seeks to 
reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, to enhance flood protection for vulnerable coastal 
populations and to improve livelihoods. It aligns with Ecuador ’s national climate change 
strategies and contributes to global initiatives such as the Global Mang rove Alliance.  
5. The project ’s components focus on increasing the area of mangroves under community -
based management, engaging the private sector in sustainable practices and strengthening 
governance frameworks. By integrating these efforts, the project aims to achieve multiple 
climate mitigation and adaptation goals, including reducing GHG emissions, protecting 
ecosystems and enhancing infrastructure resilience.  
6. Moreover, the project ’s alignment with the objectives of the Global Mangrove Alliance 
underscores its potential for knowledge -sharing and global impact. By leveraging international 
partnerships and best practices, the project aims to contribute significantly to global efforts to


---
*Page 108*
---

 
GCF/B. 39/02/Add.  10 
Page 108 
 
 
conserve and restore mangroves, ultimately benefiting both Ecuador ’s coastal communities and 
the broader global environment.  
7. The project will directly benefit 89,600 individuals through enhanced access to climate 
finance, adoption of climate -smart aquaculture practices, and mangrove restoration to mitigate 
landslide and flooding risks. Moreover, the long -term conservation and su stainable 
management of mangroves will indirectly benefit 3 .4 million  people, spanning 44  per cent  of 
coastal provinces and 20.1  per cent  of the country ’s population, as a result  of the climate -
resilient practices.  
8. The total mitigation impact from mangrove conservation, restoration and preservation  
amounts to an estimated GHG emission reduction of 732,111 tonnes of carbon dioxide 
equivalent ( t CO 2 eq) by the end of the implementation period and 4,609,470 t CO 2 eq over the 
project ’s 20 -year lifespan, equivalent to USD 3.2  per t CO 2 eq for GCF finance. This project ’s 
estimated contribution to the national ly determined contribution  (NDC ) stands at 732,111 t  CO 2 
eq or 1.2  per cent  over the project implementation period, r ising to 4,609,470 t  CO 2 eq or 7.4  per 
cent  over the 20-year project duration . 
9. The proposal include s a comprehensive budget totall ing USD 45.9 million, with a 
significant portion (USD 36.4 million ) sought from the GCF as a grant. The remaining USD 9.5 
million is anticipated to be sourced from co -financing by the Ministry of Environment, Water 
and Transition of Ecuador and the Conservation International Foundation (CI), in the form of  
both in- kind contributions and grants.  
2.2 Component -by-component analysis  
Component 1 : Mangrove areas under effective and climate -adapted management increased , 
including through community- based management  (AUSCEMs),  and with protected areas 
implementing climate adaptation plans ( total cost: USD 26  million; GCF  cost:  USD 19.5  million ) 
10. Component 1 of the project aims to increase mangrove areas under effective and 
climate -adapted management  (AUSCEMs) , including through community -based management  
and protected areas i mplementing climate adaptation plans. Despite the effectiveness of 
community -based stewardship in protecting mangroves, there are gaps in coverage that can be 
addressed through outreach and providing assistance in organizing, planning and strengthening 
relationships with government program mes that recognize  AUSCEMs. The component is crucial 
to ensure that existing AUSCEM associations have the organizational capacity to sustain their 
stewardsh ip efforts.  
11. Community -based mechanisms for mangrove conservation are often economically 
precarious ; therefore, generating stronger local economic benefits based on mangrove 
conservation is essential for ensuring sustainability. Restoration efforts under community stewardship will contribute to enhancing mangrove coverage and associated benefits.  
12. To achieve these goals, o utput 1.1 focuses on reducing flood risk for vulnerable 
populations and reducing GHG emissions , through strengthening community -based mangrove 
management. Activities include providing training, technical assistance and equipment to 
AUSCEM groups, supporting the expansion of AUSCEMs, and assisting with the application 
process for incentives under the country’s Socio Manglar  (SM) programme . 
13. Output 1.2 aims to improve livelihood activities and economically productive 
community businesses to incentivize participation in mangrove conservation and restoration. 
This involves providing technical and business development support to mangrove communit y 
associations, particularly focusing on women, youth and other vulnerable groups. In a ddition, 
financial mechanisms will be established and consolidated to support micro - and small -scale


---
*Page 109*
---

 
GCF/B. 39/02/Add.  10 
Page 109 
 
 
enterprises, including cash grants and assistance in accessing credit and investment from 
existing financial institutions.  
14. Overall, these efforts seek to strengthen community -based mangrove conservation and 
management, reduce deforestation, increase economic benefits for local communities and 
enhance resilience to climate change.  
Component 2 : The private sector becomes a transformational agent for change by reducing GHG 
emissions and providing financial support to conserve and restore mangroves that increase 
climate resilience for other coastal populations  (total cost: USD 10.2 million; GCF  cost:  USD 8.9 
million ) 
15. The project aims to engage the private sector in promoting improved shrimp production 
methods to reduce mangrove deforestation and advance mangrove conservation and 
restoration in Ecuador. CI is t he accredited entity  (AE) , and it will collaborate with Ecuador ’s 
National Conservation Agency  to provide training on sustainable shrimp production (SSP) and 
promote climate -smart shrimp (CSS) farming methods, including mangrove restoration on 
farms. CI will also engage consultants to help small-  and medium -sized shrimp farms access 
private finance.  
16. Seed funding will be provided by the Aquaculture Stewardship Council and CI to 
establish a subaccount within Ecuador ’s Socio Bosque Fund to support the S M program me for 
the incentive payment . The project aims to cataly se investment in CSS farming methods, leading 
to reduced GHG emissions, increased profitability and mangrove restoration.  
17. Technical assistance will be provided to shrimp farms for adopting SSP and CSS 
standards, with a focus on sustainable intensification and mangrove restoration. The project will facilitate partnerships and access to finance for shrimp farms transitioning to  sustainable 
practices, including through the E co Business Fund. It will also establish agreements with 
businesses to contribute to mangrove conservation and to finance the SM program me. 
18. In addition, the project will disseminate knowledge through various platforms and 
workshops, develop a marketing strategy for CSS  farming , and seek to mobilize private 
investment into the SM subaccount, supported by funds from the GCF. These efforts aim to 
strengthen private  sector engagement in mangrove conservation and restoration while 
promoting SSP practices in Ecuador.  With successful management, the SM subaccount is 
expected to create a long -term financial mechanism that attracts private sector invest ment and 
provides incentive payments for sustainable mangrove conservation and restoration.  
Component 3 : Create the enabling conditions for sustaining reductions in mangrove deforestation 
and increased mangrove restoration by strengthening  governance, climate change adaptation 
strategies, coastal management policies and legal enforcement  (total cost:  USD 6.2 million; GCF  
cost:  USD 5.5 million)  
19. Actions by local communities and the private sector to protect mangroves and maintain 
flood protection benefits as a nature- based climate change adaptation and mitigation strategy 
are contingent upon the G overnment of Ecuador establishing the necessary governance, 
regulatory and enabling conditions. These conditions are essential  for deterring and controlling 
illegal activities and ensuring that climate considerations are integrated into planning and 
investment decisions.  
20. By providing better climate change and mangrove forest information to decision -makers 
and stakeholders, particularly focusing on public entities, the project will directly contribute to 
increasing climate knowledge for decision -making, laying the groundwor k for achieving project 
outcomes related to mangrove management and climate resilience.  
21. Improved legal and regulatory capacity, focusing on planning, preparedness and 
enforcement, will support sustaining mangroves and enhancing awareness and resilience


---
*Page 110*
---

 
GCF/B. 39/02/Add.  10 
Page 110 
 
 
among beneficiaries, primarily in the eight priority municipalities of the project area. This will 
directly contribute to strengthening institutional and regulatory systems for climate -responsive 
planning and development.  
22. To achieve these objectives, the project includes several activities under o utput 3.1, such 
as monitoring mangrove condition s and socioeconomic impacts, integrating remote sensing and 
community participation for emissions reduction monitoring, and conducting socioeconomic 
impact evaluations. Output 3.2 entails harmonizing legal and regulatory frameworks at the local 
and sectoral level, incorporating climate resilience and mitigation strategies, and enforcing regulations with a focus on human rights.  
23. Activities under o utput 3.2 include supporting local governments to improve planning 
instruments that integrate climate change adaptation and mangrove management, 
strengthening regulatory frameworks and law enforcement by relevant agencies, and 
generating proposals for regu latory changes in various sectors. This will involve analys ing legal 
frameworks, facilitating discussions on legal reforms and providing training to judiciary staff. In 
addition, the project will develop an online and mobile communications application ( app) for  
reporting deforestation and other impacts on mangroves, thereby enhancing transparency and 
enforcement efforts.  
III. Assessment against investment criteria  
3.1 Impact  potential                                                                                   Scale: N/A       
24. The project aims to directly benefit 89,600 individuals, with an additional 3.4 million 
indirect beneficiaries. Furthermore, it targets an estimated GHG emission reduction of  
4,609,470 t CO 2 eq over its 20 -year duration.  
25. The AUSCEM  project  seeks to address both adaptation and mitigation challenges posed 
by climate change, particularly in vulnerable coastal regions. By leveraging the protective 
capacities of mangrove ecosystems, the project endeavo urs to yield significant benefits for both 
local communities and the global environment. This project represents a multifaceted approach 
to address ing climate change impacts at both the local and the global scale, highlighting the 
crucial role of mangrove ecosystems in fostering resilience a nd mitigating environmental 
degradation.  The project offers both adaptation and mitigation benefits, focusing on reducing 
flooding risks and enhancing livelihoods through improved mangrove management.  
26. Adaptation impacts: Direct adaptation benefits include reduced flooding risk and 
increased resilience for vulnerable populations , while there are a number of indirect adaptation 
benefits  for s elected municipalities with high mangrove cover and populations at risk of 
flooding , including : 
(a) Potential benefits for 89,600 people in mitigating flood risks , according to f lood risk 
model ling estimates;  
(b) A USD  280 million per year  reduction in e conomic asset losses caused by  extreme 
climate -related disasters ; 
(c) Approximately 3.4 million indirect beneficiaries from improved planning processes in 
mangrove -rich municipalities ; 
(d) 41,500 individuals (50  per cent  female, 50  per cent  male) expected to adopt climate-
resilient livelihoods or experience reduced flood risk ; 
(e) 156,633 hectares of mangroves protected and strengthened, covering all of Ecuador ’s 
mangroves ; and


---
*Page 111*
---

 
GCF/B. 39/02/Add.  10 
Page 111 
 
 
(f) Restoration efforts covering  protected areas, sustainable land -use program mes and 
shrimp farms.  
27. Mitigation impacts : Mitigation benefits arise from the decreased GHG emissions 
resulting from a  reduc tion  in mangrove deforestation and an increase in carbon sequestration 
from restored mangroves , amounting to an estimated GHG emission reduction of 732,111 t  CO 2 
eq during the implementation period and 4,609,470 t  CO 2 eq over the 20-year  project lifespan . 
3.2 Paradigm  shift potential                                                                     Scale: N/A   
28. This project aims to transform Ecuador ’s approach to valuing and utilizing critical 
natural infrastructure, particularly mangrove ecosystems, to protect coastal communities from 
flooding. By integrating the adaptation and mitigation benefits of mangroves into governance 
and economic frameworks, the project offers an innovative solution to reducing GHG emissions 
and enhancing the livelihoods of vulnerable populations. It addresses the current undervaluation of climate risks and ecosystem services provided by mangroves,  which is the 
result of  inad equate information, institutional coordination and private sector investment.  
29. The project employs a multifaceted strategy, including strengthening public sector 
frameworks, fostering public –private partnerships and empowering local communities, with a 
focus on leveraging the “ green infrastructure ” of mangroves. This approach is cost -effective and 
scalable, and includes  lessons, guidance and tools that can be used for other coastal 
communities threatened by climate change. Comprehensive monitoring and reporting 
mechanisms will facilitate learning and knowledge -sharing throughout the p roject, with mid -
term and final evaluations to identify lessons learned and disseminate findings.  
30. The project aims to create an enabling environment and policy framework by 
strengthening regulatory frameworks and fostering dialogue and collaboration across sectors at 
the national and subnational level. Capacity -building initiatives will empower local communities 
to participate in innovative business models and to engage in land -use planning processes with 
local government. The project emphasizes consensus -building among stakeholders through 
participatory processes, facilitating agreements for the sustainable management of mangroves. 
Private sector participation will be enhanced through support for land -use planning and the 
adoption of climate -smart practices, particularly mangrove conservation and restoration.  
31. The project focuses on scaling up successful mangrove conservation and restoration 
activities in Ecuador, addressing the lack of basic land -use planning instruments in the project 
area. Participatory planning processes will be facilitated, documented and s hared with the 
national government, with recommendations for legal and regulatory adjustments as needed.  
3.3 Sustainable development potential                                                Scale: N/A  
32. Environmental co-benefits:  The project aims to reduce deforestation, enhance 
restoration and improve management practices across Ecuador ’s entire mangrove area, which  
span s 156,633 hectares . In addition to  mitigating climate change and reducing flood risks, 
mangroves offer further  ecosystem services;  for example, t hey support biodiversity 
conservation, acting as habitats for numerous plant and bird species. Mangroves also serve as 
vital nursing grounds for fisheries, fostering the spawning, breeding and early development  of 
various fish species. Thus mangroves play a significant role in fish production and provide substantial economic value through fisheries -related ecosystem services. The total ecosystem 
service value of mangroves, including biodiversity and other services, is estimated to be substantial, providing essential benefits to local economies.


---
*Page 112*
---

 
GCF/B. 39/02/Add.  10 
Page 112 
 
 
33. Social co-benefits:  In addition to mitigating flood risks, the project will yield various 
social co -benefits, particularly for mangrove- dependent communities and associations , as 
follows : 
(a) Improved social cohesion and organizational capacity among 60 associations  with 
AUSCEMs , which  will enhance the ability of communities to engage with government 
stakeholders, resolve internal conflicts and develop additional community activities;  
(b) Enhanced mangrove management and planning are likely to integrate social and 
environmental considerations, including water management and waste disposal,  
benefiting human health and well- being ; and  
(c) Collaborative efforts to control illegal activities in mangrove areas between community -
based associations and government authorities will enhance local security, generating collateral benefits.  
34. Economic co-benefits:  Mangrove conservation and restoration offer a cost -effective 
solution for climate change adaptation, generating economic benefits for the national and local 
government, the private sector and communities by avoiding the costs of extreme climate 
events. The shrimp production sector stands to benefit by accessing new markets through 
sustainable and CSS  production, leading to increased export revenue and employment 
opportunities.  
35. Communities will experience reduced flooding, along with minimized impacts on 
infrastructure, housing, crops and livelihoods. Diversification of livelihoods and adoption of 
adaptive fishing techniques will enhance the resilience, economic stability and foo d security of 
marginalized coastal fishing communities. Mangrove ecosystems form the foundation for 
diversified local economies, with artisanal fishing playing a significant role in sustaining 
livelihoods and contributing to marine fisheries.  
36. Gender -sensitive development impact:  The project will directly contribute to 
empowering women and other vulnerable groups in economic activities and advancing their 
participation and leadership in organizations and enterprises, ensuring a gender -sensitive 
approach to development initiatives.  
3.4 Needs of the recipient                                                                         Scale: N/A  
37. Ecuador has experienced notable economic growth  from 2007 and 2018 , resulting in a 
reduc tion  in the levels of poverty and inequality and the ongoing  expan sion of the middle class. 
However, the economy ’s heavy reliance on the oil sector presents a sign ificant vulnerability, 
particularly evident during periods of low oil prices and the appreciation of the US dollar.  
38. With over 3,630 km of coastline, Ecuador ’s five coastal provinces are home to  
approximately 54  per cent  of the population. These areas face significant risks from sea level 
rise, saltwater intrusion, storm surges, flooding and storms. Coastal regions of  Ecuador are 
disproportionately affected by poverty, with 45  per cent  of the population in the project area 
living below the extreme poverty line. Unsafe housing conditions persist due to rapid 
urbanization and persistent poverty.  
39. The challenges of coastal development are compounded by the threats of climate 
change, yet Ecuador lacks an integrated plan to protect mangroves and wetlands from the 
combined impacts of sea level rise and intensified storms. The project addresses these pressing 
needs by aligning with Ecuador ’s national climate change strategy and p lan, with the aim of 
support ing the national and local government, coastal communities and crucial  economic 
sectors such as shrimp farming and fisheries.

GCF/B. 39/02/Add.  10 
Page 113 
 
 
3.5 Country ownership                                                                              Scale: N/A  
40. The project is in alignment with the NDC  in terms of contributing to both mitigation and 
adaptation goals  through the aim of protecti ng mangrove ecosystems.  The project is also  aligned 
to other national policies , including  the national climate change strategy, the conservation of the 
national forest estate, and REDD+ efforts . Moreover, the project is linked to the nation al 
measurement, reporting and verification  and monitoring and evaluation ( M&E ) systems for 
NDC im plementation ; thus the results will be agg regated into the national biennial transparency 
reports that the national governm ent will submit  under the United Nations Framework 
Convention on Climate Change .  
41. In addition, c ountry ownership is firmly established through active government  
participation in project management structures.  Both the Ministry of E nvironment, Water and 
Ecological Transition  and the Vice-ministry of A quaculture and F isheries will be part of the 
project management advisory committee and  the technical committees guiding each project 
component . This involvement enhances ownership and promotes awareness of the 
technological solutions proposed by the project, setting the foundation for replication and 
scala bility in the future .  
3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness                                                          Scale: N/A  
42. The project  provides the most value of the scenarios considered over both the project 
implementation period ( 6.5 years) and the project life span  (20 years ), and it gives a high 
benefit -to-cost ratio (8 :1). The project is expected to generate USD 299 million of economic 
value over and above the “without project ” scenario over the 20-year project life span . As such , 
the project as designed provides excellent value for money because it will generate significant 
benefits to society over both the implementation period ( 6.5 years) and the expected project 
lifespan (20 years).  
IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies  
4.1 Environmental and social safeguards  
43.  The project aims to achieve GHG emission reduction s while providing flood protection 
and enhancing the livelihoods of the communities through coordinated efforts to protect and restore the mangrove ecosystem in Ecuador. The project interventions are intended to have 
positive impacts on people and the environment. Any unintended adverse impacts are expected 
to be minor and site-specific , and can be managed through careful design and standard 
mitigation measures. T he environmental co -benefits  due to reduced deforestation, restoration 
and management of the man grove ecosystem include enhanced habitat s, particularly for bird 
and fish species, and enhanced biodiversity and ecosystem services. T he social co -benefits 
include reduced flood risks, improved capacity for mangrove management and better human health and well -being.  
44. Environmental and social risk category and safeguards instrument. The project has 
been classified as environmental and social (E&S) risk c ategory B. The Secretariat confirms the 
categorization and that it is within the AE’s E&S risk accreditation level. Since the interventions 
are several and located in multiple sites involving various communities and development 
partners, and are not yet identified, the AE has prepared an e nvironmental and s ocial impact 
assessment and an e nvironmental and s ocial management  plan (ESMP) which together identif y 
the potential risks and impacts and possible mitigation measures and set out the procedures for 
assessing and managing E&S  risks and impacts of the individual interventions (subprojects).


---
*Page 114*
---

 
GCF/B. 39/02/Add.  10 
Page 114 
 
 
45. Compliance with GCF ’s Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Standards  
46. The following paragraphs describe the project’s compliance with the GCF interim ESS 
standards:  
47. ESS1 : Assessment and management of e nvironmental and s ocial risks and 
impacts.  An environmental and social impact assessment  and ESMP (included as a nnex 6 to the 
funding proposal) provide a summary of the project activities and outcomes and the applicable 
legal and institutional framework, the biophysical and social environment, the identified 
potential risks and impacts, the mitigation measures and action plan , and the implementation 
and monitoring mechanisms by which the project will be implemented. The main risk pertains 
to potential restriction or loss of access  to mangrove resources  for some groups or individuals 
resulting in economic displacement. However, economic displacement, if any, is expected to be 
minor because  the planning and management of mangrove areas will be undertaken by the 
communities themselves , who are the traditional users and beneficiaries of the resource.  
48. ESS2 : Labou r and w orking conditions.  The project will mostly involve the community 
members themselves implement ing the activities under the project. However , those who will be 
working on the subprojects may be exposed to drowning hazards, poisonous animals and disease vectors. Mitigation measures will include assessing the health and safety risks to 
workers and developing prevention and emergency response planning for these activities. The 
project’s screening procedure also includes questions regarding whet her the subproject has a 
risk of potentially involving or lead to working conditions that do not meet national or 
international labo ur laws and regulations. In this regard, the project will ensure the health and 
safety of subproject -related workers and protect workers’ rights. Further, the project also 
excludes activities that might include the use of forced labo ur or child labo ur.  
49. ESS3 : Resource efficiency and p ollution prevention . The project is not expected to 
contribute to pollution but rather will support farms to reduce the use of materials that could 
generate and discharge pollutants to the ecosystem. Hence, the project’s activities are expected 
to lessen the pollution caused by the shrimp aquaculture farms and improve their resource 
efficiency ap proaches. The project’s activities include technical assistance for the development 
and promotion of CSS  aquaculture practices.  
50. ESS4 : Community health, safety and s ecurity. The mangrove 
restoration/reforestation activity of the project may expose community members to physical 
hazards, poisonous animals and disease vectors,  given that mangrove forests are home to some 
potentially dangerous animals and vectors that may result in  diseases such as malaria, dengue, 
leptospirosis and vibriosis. Community health, safety and security risk assessments will be 
conducted, and community health, safety and security plans will be implemented on each of the 
subprojects. The measures that will be implemented include first aid training for all project staff 
and guidance on how to address  communities’ safety related concerns.  
51. ESS5 : Land acquisition and i nvoluntary resettlement.  The AE has determined that 
the project would not involve acquisition of land but could potentially result to restriction or loss of access for some groups or individuals to mangrove resources. A process framework for 
restriction of access to natural resources is included as procedural guidance should community 
associations put in place restrictions on access to the mangrove resources for both members 
and non- members of these associations. The framework includes eligibility criteria,  measures to 
assist affected people , and a conflict resolution and complaint mechanism.  
52. ESS6 : Biodiversity conservation and s ustainable management of l iving natural 
resources . The AE has determined the applicability of ESS6 given that the project will support 
various forest use and conservation activities that may take place in and/or around protected 
areas and buffer zones. The project is not expected to result in the introduction of invasive 
species of flora and fauna , since the project aims to use only native species and enhance the 
ecosystem services that mangrove forests provide. The proj ect includes mangrove restoration


---
*Page 115*
---

 
GCF/B. 39/02/Add.  10 
Page 115 
 
 
activities using native and locally sourced species within the areas managed by the AUSCEMs, 
some of which are within protected areas. Additional restoration activities will also take place 
on private shrimp farms. The project will sign conservation agreem ents with the local 
communities that are to be supported by the project, particularly those that will oversee 
establishing forestry units and conduct reforestation activities. Assessment o f the 
environmental, social and economic viability of the restoratio n/reforestation activities will be 
conducted to ensure that the activities are not only technical feasible but are environmentally 
appropriate, socially beneficial and economically viable.  
53. The application of the GCF Indigenous Peoples  Policy takes into consideration Afro -
Ecuadorians and cholo artisanal fisher communities who are located within the project area . In 
addition to mainstreaming their engagement throughout the main funding proposal  documents, 
the Plan for Afro -descendent Communities and Cholos Pescadores  provides a specific 
instrument for ensuring that the rights of th ese groups are considered and strengthened 
throughout and beyond the project life cycle. In the preparation of the funding proposal , 
consultations were undertaken with both Indigenous Peoples and local communities, with 
outcomes included in the plan. In line with their roles and functions, the Indigenous Peoples 
Advisory Group is available to provide advice to the AE  and executing entities  (EEs) . 
54. ESS8 : Cultural heritage.  The project is not expected to implement activities that might 
affect cultural heritage. As part of its exclusion list, it will also not support activities that will “involve the removal, alteration, or disturbance of any non- replicable or critical cultur al 
heritage, or the use of intangible cultural heritage without the f ree, prior and i nformed consent 
of the communities to whom it belongs.”  
55. Sexual exploitation, abuse and h arassment (SEAH) . The revised GCF Environmental 
and Social Policy adopted by decision B.BM -2021/18 requires safeguarding from SEAH  in GCF-
financed activities. The AE provided information on SEAH safeguards during the proposal 
development and outlined the SEAH risks in the ESMP, as well as providing  some SEAH 
mitigation measures in the gender action plan. The AE is committed to ensuring a safe, trusted and respectful environment within its  organization and global offices, as well as in its  
program mes and projects. This is in alignment with the CI SEAH policy which is applicable to its 
projects. The project recognizes potential SEAH risks , especially community engagements that 
will involve working directly with marginalized and vulnerable groups/communities. The AE 
will ensure that , in cases where SEAH incidents occur (or have a high probability of occurring), 
the rights and dignity of survivors are upheld in a confidential and gender -respons ive manner. 
The AE has therefore identified potential SEAH risks and mitigation measures  in the ESMP . 
56. Implementation arrangements . The project will have a p roject steering committee 
that will provide guidance and support management regarding  project implementation. A 
project management advisory committee will also be established to provide advice on technical 
matters. CI  Ecuador will be the EE for all activities of this project and will be responsible for 
project execution, management and reporting of project’s activities. A p roject management unit 
will also be established and will be responsible for overall coordination, management and 
implementation of the project on the ground. The project will have dedicated full- time staff, 
including a s afeguards manager and a gender manager among other full -time staff who will be 
responsible for  safeguards and gender -related matters . An e stuary field unit will be established 
in each of the project’s four target estuaries to be directly involved in the daily activities of the local associations.  
57. Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure.  The AE has engaged 
stakeholders through workshops and targeted meetings at the national and local level. The consultation process involved key project stakeholders such as the men and women of local 
mangrove associations and other disadvantaged or vulnerable stakeholders. Community 
organizations managing AUSCEMs were also involved. The issues raised in the consultation 
process were considered in the development of the project. A stakeholder engagement plan has


---
*Page 116*
---

 
GCF/B. 39/02/Add.  10 
Page 116 
 
 
been  prepared to identify stakeholders, define participation strategies, establish procedures and 
methodologies, and identify issues and concerns that could affect project implementation.  
58. Grievance redress mechanism (GRM).  The project has described a project -level GRM 
that will be promoted during the awareness -raising and capacity -building activities of the 
project. It describes  the types of complaints that can be addressed, who can raise a complaint, 
the contact points at the community level, and the responsible authorities in handling 
complaints. The GRM also describe s the ability for stakeholders to access CI’s Ethics Hotline that 
employees, grantees and other partners and beneficiaries c an access to report incidents 
anonymously. The GRM also allows for stakeholders to access the GCF’s Independent Redress 
Mechanism.  In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the GCF I ndigenous Peoples Focal 
Point will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.  
4.2 Gender policy  
59. The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal , and 
therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy. The assessment report 
presents information on the differential impact of and vulnerability to  climate change for  
women and men in the targeted project area. The gender assessment is based on secondary 
data, as well as  consultation workshops that  were held  between 2016 and 2019. However, t he 
majority of the secondary data is from outdated sources ( i.e. 2010 –2012 ). The S ecretariat 
therefore recommends that , during early project implementation , the AE may need to make 
some revisions to the documents in annex 8 of the funding proposal  if there are any major 
changes in the demographics and/or socioeconomic statuses that  may have a bearing on this 
specific project.  
60. The gender action plan  for the project outlines various interventions in order to ensure 
that anticipated gender outcomes are realized. The emphasis  is on interventions that will aim at 
increasing women ’s participation in ensuring diversified and climate -resilient livelihood 
options in mangroves , as well as increasing women’s leadership, skills building within 
associations and gender quotas , among other  thing s. The Secretariat recommends and suggests 
that the AE include the following  provisions : (i) engage local women and fisherwomen in 
participatory project planning and implementation , including utilizing their knowledge of 
marine and coastal resources i n assessments, and their traditional practices in natural resource 
management; (ii) ensure that women -led businesses  have improved financial and market access 
and profitability, and address barriers within families and households by involving men; (iii) 
build critical awareness among women and disadvantaged groups on their rights as women and 
fisherfolk; and (iv) define common goals and develop str ategic alliances between women ’s 
groups and other organizations/associations for more effective advocacy for po licy change. 
Gender balance should be considered when forming stakeholder groups; this may involve 
including female minority leaders and managers to encourage participation and capacity -
building. Also, the AE needs to ensure that financial leakage is eliminat ed and that financial 
resources actually reach the needy, poor, vulnerable and disadvantaged communities including 
women and girls.  
4.3 Risks  
4.3.1.  Overall programme assessment ( medium risk)  
61. The project aims to coordinate efforts across the public, private and community sectors 
to protect and restore mangroves in Ecuador. This initiative plans to reduce mangrove loss, 
resulting in a reduction in GHG emission s amounting to 4.6 Mt  CO 2 eq over the project lifespan . 
It will also accelerate mangrove recovery to enhance flood protection for 89,600 people and


---
*Page 117*
---

 
GCF/B. 39/02/Add.  10 
Page 117 
 
 
improve the livelihoods of 41,500 people. The project activities are structured around three 
main components: (i) reducing flood risks and increasing resilience while also reducing GHG 
emissions and increasing sequestration; (ii) engaging the private sector in emission reduction s 
and providing financial support for mangrove conservation; and (iii) creating conditions to 
sustain reductions in mangrove deforestation and increase restoration efforts by strengthening 
governance and coastal management policies. The project, with a total cost of USD 45.93 million, 
will be funded by USD 36.4 million from the GCF as grant, with additional co -financing total ling 
USD 9.53 million (grants and in -kind) for a co -financing ratio of 1:0.26 .  
4.3.2.  Accredited entity /executing entity capability to execute the current programme 
(low  risk)  
62. CI, through its CI -GCF agency, will serve as the A E with CI Ecuador overseeing project 
execution. With a track record in conservation spanning three decades and covering Galapagos, 
marine/coastal regions, Andes Choco and the Amazon, CI has a history of supporting the 
creation and management of protected areas and collaborat ing with artisanal fisher 
associations for sustainable resource management. GCF has collaborated with CI through 
previous projects ( i.e. FP158 and FP026) in addressing mitigation, adaptat ion and sustainable 
livelihood s in Africa.  
4.3.3.  Programme -specific execution risks (medium risk)  
63. Security risk : The Secretariat  note s that there are reports of threat s from criminal 
gangs engaging in violent crimes and illicit activities across Ecuador. The coastal areas, 
particularly Guayaquil, are reportedly entangled in the cocaine trade, with drug traffickers 
exploiting weak law enforcement and port vulnerabilities to smuggle drugs, exacerbating 
security concerns in the country, potentially disrupting project implementation. The AE also 
acknowledges these challenges posed by criminal gangs operating in remote mangrove areas 
and the exploitation of Ecuador ’s coastal a reas for cocaine trafficking, underscoring potential 
impacts on project activities. In its mitigation plan, the AE has developed a s ecurity plan ( annex 
6, ESMP a ppendix 11), and CI ’s Safety and Security Director will conduct a security assessment 
and develop a mitigation plan during project startup. The mitigation measures will include pre -
activity community engagement, hostile environment awareness training and ensuring easy 
identification of CI’s  staff .  
64. Political instability:  The political landscape in Ecuador has been characterized by 
instability in the past and remains unstable following the October 2023 election. Newly elected 
President Noboa was voted into office without a majority in the National Assembly. To secure political stability, the President formed a coalition with two other political parties in t he radical 
left and conservatives. Nonetheless, CI ’s 20 years of experience operating in Ecuador ’s political 
context provide a robust foundation for  implementing mitigation measures. CI has strong 
relations hips  with local and provincial government officials , and it is expected that this will help 
to deliver  proactive engagement with various stakeholders , including local communities, project 
partners and relevant authorities , to help address political challenges.  
65. Project viability (and concessionality):  The use of grants (i.e. 100 per cent 
concessionality) is justified given the country context where public and private funding for 
mangrove conservation and restoration efforts is limited and insufficient. Vulnerable 
populations in Ecuador are directly exposed to heightened flood risks exacerbated by climate 
change , and they lack adaptive capacity due to poverty. Grant funding is therefore warranted to 
create the enabling conditions for sustain ing capacity and incentiviz ing private sector 
investments in mangroves, ensuring the integration of climate resilience measures and fostering long -term sustainability.


---
*Page 118*
---

 
GCF/B. 39/02/Add.  10 
Page 118 
 
 
4.3.4.  Compliance r isk (medium risk)  
66. Ecuador is not currently subject to United Nations Security Council resolutions, nor is it 
currently identified on the Financial Action Task Force lists of high -risk jurisdictions or 
jurisdictions under increased monitoring relating to a nti-money laundering (AML)  and 
countering the f inancing of terrorism (CFT).  
67. The funding proposal noted a risk of criminal gangs operating in project areas and rated 
the probability as “medi um”. The m itigating measures described in the  security plan includ e 
staff training, mapping of high -risk areas, and a country threat and vulnerability assessment, as 
well as cooperation with local partners and authorities. The funding proposal also noted that 
project areas are located along a drug  shipping route. The AE provided further information on 
its AML/CFT controls in a nnex 20 to the funding proposal and confirmed that its AML/CFT 
policies include sanctions screening of subgrantees and EE (CI Ecuador) employees.  
68. Furthermore, as the political and security situation in Ecuador has evolved since the 
proposal was first submitted, the AE confirmed that the s ecurity plan has been updated 
accordingly, with additional security costs including in the budget.  
69. Given the aforementioned risks, as well as the ongoing uncertainty within Ecuador, this 
project is deemed to be medium risk from a compliance perspective.  
4.3.5.  GCF portfolio concentration risk ( low risk)   
70. In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in 
terms of result area and single proposal is immaterial.  
4.3.6.  Recommendation  
71. It is recommended that the Board consider the above factors in its decision.

4.5 Results monitoring and reporting  
78. As a cross- cutting project, the funding proposal  aims to achieve a combination of 
mitigation and adaptation outcomes spanning three GCF result s areas : forestry and land use for 
mitigation ; most vulnerable people and communities , and ecosystems and ecosystems services 
for adaptation. The expected mitigation outcome is  an estimated GHG emission reduction of 
4,609,470 t  CO 2 eq over the project’s 20 -year lifespan  and 732,111 t  CO 2 eq by the end of the 
implementation period . The expected adaptation beneficiaries  amount to 89,600 and 3.4 million 
direct and indirect beneficiaries respectively. The AE submitted the necessary annex to 
demonstrate the calculation method s used for these estimates.  
79. The theory of change has been improved and refined to clearly articulate the results 
chain that connects the proposed activities, outputs, outcomes and impact. It covers pathways 
that will lead to the expected climate outcomes and co -benefits . 
80. The logical framework is consistent with the requirements of t he Integrated Results 
Management Framework . The project is expected to reali ze its paradigm shift potential through 
a combination of building community knowledge and resources for mangrove management and 
livelihoods, strengthening the enabling environment for mangrove management, and 
strengthening collaboration between the private sector  and government on mangrove 
protection and restoration. Core i ndicator 2 (adaptation beneficiaries), s upplementary indicator 
2.1 (adoption of climate- resilient livelihood optio ns), supplementary indicator 3.1 (change in 
expected losses), c ore indicator 4 (natural resources brought under improved management 
practices), supplementary indicator 4.1(natural resources restored) and c ore indicator 1 (GHG 
emissions reduced) have all been incorporated into the logical framework and relevant means 
of verification, targets and assumptions have been specified.  
81. The M&E p lan, which includes indicator -specific approaches for data collection and data 
sources, and an evaluation plan, are well provided for in the project budget and AE fees 
respectively. The budget allocation for the M&E activities is  consistent with the requirements of 
the GCF Evaluation Policy.  
4.6 Legal assessment  
82. The AMA was signed with the A E on 13 July 2017, and was amended and restated on 15 
August 2022, which became effective on 17 August 2022, for the second accreditation term.  
83. The A E has provided a legal certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal 
approvals,  and it has the capacity and authority to implement the p roject pursuant to clause 
4.13 of the AMA.  
84. The proposed p roject will be implemented in Ecuador, a country in which GCF is not 
provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF is not 
protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be further

GCF/B. 39/02/Add.  10 
Page 120 
 
 
assessed. The GCF Secretariat provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities and a 
background note to the Government of the Republic of Ecuador on 23 February 2023.  
85. It is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following 
conditions:  
(a) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board  approval ; and  
(b) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat .

 Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment  of 
FP235 
Proposal name:  Mangroves for climate: Public, Private and Community 
Partnerships for Mitigation and Adaptation in Ecuador  
Accredited entity:  Conservation International Foundation  
Country:  Ecuador  
Project/programme size:  Small 
I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel  
1.1 Overview  
1. The Mangroves for c limate: Public , Private and Community Partnerships for Mitigation 
and Adaptation in Ecuador  project  is a c ross -cutting project aimed at strengthening the 
resilience of Ecuador’s coastal areas to the negative impacts of climate change, while also 
significantly reducing greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. Over a n implementation  period of six-
and-a-half years, this small -sized  project will bring together the public, private, and community 
sectors to focus on the conservation and restoration of mangrove ecosystems, which are crucial 
for coastal protection,  biodiversity , and local livelihoods. This project meets the urgent need for 
climate adaptation and mitigation in regions prone to extreme weather and rising sea levels, 
providing direct benefits to Ecuador ’s most vulnerable coastal communities.  
2. The project interventions are centered  around three main components:  
(a) Community empowerment : Enhance the management and restoration of mangroves 
through community -led initiatives, thereby improving local livelihoods and ensuring 
sustainable ecosystem services;  
(b) Private -sector engagement : Mobilize the shrimp farming sector to adopt and finance 
climate -smart practices that align with mangrove conservation efforts ; and  
(c) Create and e nabling environment : Develop and enforce policies that integrate 
mangrove conservation into broader climate strategies, supported by improved 
governance and legal frameworks . 
3. The execution of the proposed project is expected to i) protect and manage 156,633 ha 
of mangrove ecosystems; ii) reduce GHG  emissions by 4.6 million ton nes of carbon dioxide 
equivalent  (Mt CO 2 eq) over its 20 -year lifespan ; and iii) enhance the climate resilience of 
89,600 people by reducing flood risks through ecosystem -based adaptations.  
4. The total budget for the proposed project is USD 45.9 million. A substantial portion of 
this budget , USD 36.4 million , is requested as a grant from GCF. Co -financing of USD 9.5 million  
will be provided by Ecuador’s Ministry of the Environment, Water and Ecological Transition 
(Ministerio del Ambiente, Agua y T ransición del Ecuador ( MAATE )), and Conservation 
International (CI)  in the form of grants and in -kind contributions.  
5. This project is vital not only for its environmental , economic , and social benefits but also 
as a model for integrated, multisectoral climate action. It aligns with the  national climate 
strategies of Ecuador and supports global efforts under the United Nations Framework 
Convention on Climate Change and its Paris Agreement.


---
*Page 122*
---

 
GCF/B. 39/02/Add. 10 
Page 122 
 
 
   
 6. With CI as the accredited entity (AE) and e xecuting entity  (EE) , the proposed project will 
be implemented through a structured management approach involving a steering committee, a 
management unit, and localized implementation teams, ensuring efficient delivery and 
adherence to the planned outcomes.  
1.2 Impact potential        Scale:  High  
7. The project aims to significantly reduce vulnerability for the 89,600 direct and 3.4 
million indirect project beneficiaries, which represents about 1.8  per cent  and 20.1  per cent  of 
Ecuador ’s coastal and total populations, respectively. This wide reach focuses on the most 
vulnerable groups, particularly in impoverished rural coastal areas . However, d etailing 
strategies to ensure that the project benefits reach the most marginalized people within these 
groups, including tailored measures for varying vulnerab ility levels, could have enhance d the 
project ’s adaptation impact . 
8. From a climate -change mitigation perspective, the proposed project can reduce 4.6 Mt  
CO 2 eq over its 20 -year lifespan, primarily through the restoration and conservation of 
mangrove forests, which are significant carbon sinks . 
9. By prioritizing natural mangrove barriers , the proposed project avoids the long -term 
liabilities of climate -change -vulnerable constructions . Mangroves ’ inherent resilience and flood 
mitigation capabilities boost the adaptive capacity  of the exposed communities.  The 
continuation of activities initiated by the proposed project  could  integrate hybrid approaches, 
combining green and gr ey infrastructure where natural solutions might fall short .  
10. The proposed project directly causes GHG emission reductions from mangrove 
deforestation and carbon enhancement through restoration. Both activities combined aim to 
result in  a mitigation impact of 732,11 t CO 2 eq during the project’s six-and-a-half year 
implementation and 4.6 Mt CO 2 eq during its  20-year lifespan. The proposal includes a clear 
project method and details the calculations  behind the proposed project , clarifies measurement, 
reporting and v erification (MRV) at the project scale as well as how it is link ed to the national 
MRV system.  
11. Furthermore, t he proposed project indirectly supports low -emission practices by 
promoting sustainable shrimp -farming techniques within the private sector . 
12. The proposed project will bolster institutional frameworks and regulatory systems by 
embedding climate resilience in local and national governance. This involves improving the 
community management of mangroves and supporting legal frameworks that encourage 
private -sector conservation efforts.  
13. The proposed project promotes the generation and use of climate information in 
decision -making by partnering with scientific entities like the International Research Cent re on 
El Niño  Southern Oscillation . However, e stablishing robust feedback mechanisms to adapt and 
update practices based on the latest climate data could have further solidif ied this aspect.  
14. By emphasizing community -based management and partnerships, the project raises 
awareness and adaptive capacity among local populations. Educating communities on climate threats and sustainable practices is key to the project’s  strategy, empowering stakeholders to 
actively engage in conservation and adaptation efforts . 
15. Overall, the independent Technical Advisory Panel (iTAP) considers the impact potential 
for both adaptation and mitigation of the proposed project to be h igh. 
1.3 Paradigm shift potential    Scale:  Medium  to High


---
*Page 123*
---

 
GCF/B. 39/02/Add. 10 
Page 123 
 
 
   
 16. The proposed project advocates a collaborative approach, engaging public, private, and 
community partnerships in mangrove management and restoration. By integrating the 
adaptation and mitigation benefits of mangrove ecosystems into economic and governance 
frameworks, the proposed  project will  create new market opportunities, especially in the 
shrimp aquaculture sector, to promote sustainable practices.  
17. The project ’s theory of change is coherent and well -structured, focusing on increasing 
mangrove coverage for carbon sequestration and enhanced coastal resilience, directly 
supporting global low -carbon development pathways.  
18. Furthermore, the iTAP welcomes the inclusion in  activity 3.1.1. of an explicit system for 
MRV of mangrove cover and carbon stocks, including remote sensing, community participation 
and making subgrants to competitively selected universities or n on-governmental organizations 
to collaboratively generate geospatial data and capacity that will be integrated both into the 
project monitoring and e valuation (M&E) plan and the national REDD+ MRV systems.  This 
enhanced capacity should form an important building bloc k for any future private and/or public 
sector efforts to mobilize resources based on mangrove carbon sequestration.  
19. The M&E plan targe ts the ecological, social, and economic impacts of the proposed 
project. However, the documentation (the f unding proposal and its a nnex 11) lacks extensive 
details on this plan. Improving this aspect, notably establishing a clear and fit -for-purpose 
feedback mechanism, could better ensure that lessons learned are captured and effectively 
disseminated, promoting adaptive management and replication in other contexts.  
20. The proposed project involves creating a USD 4 million  endowment in the Socio Manglar 
subaccount  (a new financing window under the Socio Bosque Fund) , securing long -term 
financial support for mangrove conservation. This strategic financial planning ensures the 
continuation of project benefits beyond the funding period. However, ongoing contributions and 
financial sustainability will require continuous reinforcement.  
21. The proposed project promotes new business activities and markets, particularly in 
climate -smart shrimp farming. The potential for market creation could have been  further 
expanded by exploring additional ecosystem services provided by mangroves that could be 
monetized  (if the legal and regulatory framework in Ecuador allows it ). By incorporating 
mangrove conservation into shrimp -farming practices, the project alters incentives for market 
participants by decreasing the environmental impact of shrimp farming,  a major contributor to 
mangrove loss. Increasing awareness and highlighting the benefits of these changes among the 
broader industry could further shift market incentives.  In addition, greater diversification of 
economic activities could also offer communities greater flexibility, which would not only help 
reduce their dependence on climate -sensitive sectors, but would also increase their resilience to 
adverse climatic conditions, including extreme events such as intense rainfall.  
22. The proposed project will address barriers such as the lack of coordinated management 
and inadequate enforcement of environmental regulations . However, barriers such as those 
relating to communities ’ legal rights seem to be beyond the project ’s reach. Furthermore, long -
term financing depends to some extent on private -sector participation, and the project ’s 
strategy for achieving this is lacking in detail.  
23. Overall , the iTAP rates the paradigm shift potential of the proposed project as medium  
to high . 
1.4 Sustainable development potential     Scale:  High  
24. The proposed project demonstrates significant potential for improving environmental 
quality and conservation. It emphasizes the restoration and preservation of mangrove ecosystems, which are vital for carbon sequestration, biodiversity conservation, and acting as


---
*Page 124*
---

 
GCF/B. 39/02/Add. 10 
Page 124 
 
 
   
 natural defen ces against coastal erosion and flooding. This aligns with Sustainable Development 
Goal (SDG ) 13 (Climate a ction) and 15 (Life on l and). The project aims to reduce 4.6 Mt  CO 2 eq 
over its duration, thereby enhancing air quality and soil stability through increased mangrove 
coverage.  Given that the impacts of the project’ s planned activities on air quality are less well 
documented, the M&E  plan could incorporate this aspect.  
25. The proposed project is designed to directly benefit around 41,500 individuals  by 
supporting climate -resilient livelihoods through improved fisheries management and other 
ecosystem services provided by mangroves. It places special emphasis on gender -specific 
impacts, aiming to empower women by improving their access to resources and  decision -
making processes, aligning with SDG 5 (Gender e quality). However, ongoing efforts in gender -
focused capacity -building and ensuring equitable access to benefits are cruc ial. 
26. This project also promotes economic externalities by creating jobs, enhancing local 
industries (especially fisheries and ecotourism), and encouraging sustainable practices such as 
climate -smart shrimp farming. It is expected to bolster local and national e conomic resilience, 
aligning with SDG 8 (Decent w ork and e conomic growth). By integrating local industries with 
conservation efforts, the project aims to establish a sustainable development model that can 
attract private investment, thereby enhancing the financial sustainability of conservation 
initiatives. Achieving long -term economic benefits will require strong partnerships and ongoing 
engagement with the private sector. Furthermore, to foster innovation and skills development 
within the local job market, the proposed project could have also consider ed strength ening 
fram eworks for collaboration between industry and academia.  
27. The proposed project caters to the needs of both women and men by promoting gender 
equality through inclusive participation in activities and decision -making processes. It places 
special emphasis on empowering women in the community, acknowledging their ro le in the 
economy , and in managing natural resources. Interventions are specifically designed to reduce 
women ’s vulnerability to climate -change impacts by improving their access to resources and 
information. However, the proposed project could also have developed more tailored 
interventions that address the specific vulnerabilities of women in coastal communities, potentially by expanding gende r-focused educational and economic opportunities.  
28.  The proposed project  effectively addresses multiple facets of sustainable development, 
including environmental protection, social inclusion, and economic growth, with a particular 
focus on gender equality. To fully realize these potentials, continuous monitoring and adaptive 
management, robust local participation, and sustained funding and technical support will be 
essential.  
29. Overall, the iTAP rates the sustainable development  potential of the proposed project as 
high . 
1.5 Needs of the recipient       Scale:  High  
30. Ecuador ’s coastal region, especially its extensive mangrove ecosystems, faces significant 
climate hazards  such as sea -level rise, extreme El Niño events, and increased precipitation 
variability. These factors amplify coastal flooding risks, endangering local communities and 
economic activities that rely heavily on fisheries and aquaculture . This vulnerability is 
intensified by the ongoing deforestation of mangroves, which are crucial for flood control and 
erosion prevention. The project ’s focus on restoring and con serving mangrove areas directly 
addresses these vulnerabilities and risks.  
31. The proposed project focuses on areas that encompass a significant portion of Ecuador ’s 
coastal population, which is directly threatened by climate -induced flooding. Approximately 3.4 
million people in coastal provinces are indirectly affected by these climate risks, with 
substantial assets at stake, including the shrimp aquaculture industr y, a major economic driver


---
*Page 125*
---

 
GCF/B. 39/02/Add. 10 
Page 125 
 
 
   
 of the targeted coastal communities. Thus, the project ’s interventions are essential in protecting 
these populations and economic resources.  
32. The project identifies and aims to support groups particularly vulnerable to climate 
impacts, integrating strategies that address their needs into the broader national climate and 
development frameworks. However, the effectiveness of these strategies should be closely 
evaluated to ensure that they result in tangible benefits.  
33. The target ed regions experience high poverty rates and limited economic 
diversification, making them more vulnerable to climate impacts. By enhancing mangrove 
ecosystems that support fisheries and local economies, the proposed project promotes 
sustainable livelihoods and economic development, thereby reducing the socioeconomic 
vulnerability of these communities.  
34. The project is well -aligned with the needs of Ecuador ’s climate -vulnerable coastal 
populations, addressing both immediate and long -term climate change  related risks. Its 
comprehensive approach to enhancing both human and ecological resilience is promising.  
35. Therefore, the iTAP evaluates the need of the recipient for the proposed project as high.  
1.6 Country ownership       Scale:  High  
36. The proposed project directly supports Ecuador ’s objectives for low -emission and 
climate -resilient development as outlined in its n ational adaptation plan and n ationally 
determined contributions. It aims to help achieve Ecuador's n ationally determined contribution  
targets by reducing GHG emissions and promoting community -managed mangrove 
conservation through the Agreements for Sustainable Use and Custody of the Mangrove 
Ecosystem.  
37. The Organic Environmental Code of Ecuador and local regulations offer a solid policy 
foundation for the proposed project. However, these policies require strengthening to ensure 
effective mangrove conservation and restoration. The project aims to foster a supportive environment for reducing mangrove deforestation and enhancing restoration through improved 
governance and climate adaptation strategies.  
38. CI has extensive experience in managing climate -change initiatives. Alongside local 
partners, they have participated in previous mangrove conservation efforts, further enhancing 
their credibility and capability.  The proposed project offsets  a new opportunity to enhance local 
capacities further to ensure long -term sustainability and scalability of mangrove conservation 
and restoration initiatives in the country . 
39. The project development process included consultations with civil society, local 
communities, the private sector , and other stakeholders, with a focus on gender equality and 
sustainable livelihoods. It intends to maintain engagement with these groups throughout the 
project, adhering to environmental and social safeguards. However, creating more explicit 
mechanisms for continuous stakeholder input and feedback during implementation could  
improve adaptiveness and inclusiveness.  
40. The project engages in in-country institutions such as MAATE, along with local 
governments and community organizations, fostering local ownership and accountability. 
Strengthening these local institutions to effectively manage increased responsibilities will be 
crucial for succe ssful project outcomes.  
41. The proposed project is designed to complement and synergize with other national and 
international initiatives. This ensures that the project ’s activities are integrated and contribute 
to broader conservation and climate -resilience efforts.


---
*Page 126*
---

 
GCF/B. 39/02/Add. 10 
Page 126 
 
 
   
 42. The proposed project showcases strong country ownership by aligning with national 
priorities, receiving institutional support, engaging stakeholders, and integrating with national 
systems.  The track record of the AE/ executing entity  further reinforces this assessment.  
43. Overall, the iTAP considers the country ownership of the proposed project to be high.  
1.7 Efficiency and effectiveness    Scale:  Medium  to High  
44. The proposed financial structure, with a USD 36.4 million  GCF grant and total funding of 
USD 45.9 million  (co-financing ratio is 1: 0.21) , aims to tackle major obstacles like insufficient 
financing for long -term mangrove conservation and community engagement. However, the 
heavy dependence on grant funding indicates a need for a more diversified financial approach to 
ensure sustainability after the project ’s completion.  
45. The project’s reliance on grants reflects high concessionality, which is essential due to 
its focus on vulnerable communities and ecosystem services that offer public benefits. However, 
the proposed project could have more clearly outline d the steps taken to minimize the level of 
concessionality needed to ensure viability.  
46. The proposed project is designed  to leverage additional private and public investment 
rather than replace it. Specifically, it seeks to attract private- sector investment in sustainable 
shrimp farming and mangrove conservation. However, it would benefit from clearer metrics on 
the expected investment amounts and sources.  
47. The project expects to reduce 4.6 Mt  CO 2 eq over its lifetime, with an investment cost of 
roughly USD 10 per t  CO 2 eq based on total funding. To fully evaluate its relative efficiency, this 
cost -effectiveness could have been  more explicitly compared to similar projects.  
48. The proposed project shows a commitment to long -term financial sustainability and 
technological integration, particularly through the use of the best available technologies and 
practices in mangrove restoration and shrimp farming. Furthermore, the M&E methodology on 
which MAATE, universities and community monitors will be trained goes beyond measuring the 
area replanted with propagules, and the survival rate of propagules, to include the complex 
hydrological dynamics of mangrove ecosystems as a contributor to mangrove losses and gains. 
This reflects global best practice in mangrove monitoring, and will hopefully be shared by CI 
with other AEs overseeing GCF projects involving mangrove restoration.  However, providing 
more detail on financial plann ing for the post -GCF phases would offer stronger assurance of 
long -term viability.  
49. Overall, the iTAP assesses the efficiency and effectiveness of the proposed project as  
medium to  high.  
II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel  
50. The proposed project is well -structured with a solid theory of change to ensure long -
term results and impact. In order to improve it, the iTAP suggests that the AE takes into account 
the following aspects:  
(a) Diversif ication of  financial strategies for long -term sustainability : While the 
project ’s dependence on grant funding is understandable given its focus, it is crucial to 
develop a more diversified financial approach. This c ould  involve establishing 
partnerships with private -sector investors, and exploring other sustainable financing 
mechanisms. Providing clearer metrics on expected investment amounts and sources 
will strengthen financial planning and ensure the project ’s viability beyond its initial 
funding period ;


---
*Page 127*
---

 
GCF/B. 39/02/Add. 10 
Page 127 
 
 
   
 (b) Promot ion of economic diversification to enhance long -term resilience  and 
mitigation impact : To further enhance the project ’s impact  and financial viability , it is 
essential to promote economic diversification within the target ed regions. This can be 
accomplished by developing and supporting alternative livelihood opportunities beyond 
fisheries and shrimp farming. By fostering a more diversified local economy, the 
proposed project can reduce communities’  dependence on climate -sensitive sectors and 
increase their resilience to adverse climatic conditions. Additionally, providing training 
and resources for these alternative economic activities can help create more stable and 
sustainable income sources for l ocal populations ; and  
(c) Integrat ion of  comprehensive monitoring , evaluation and learning plans : 
Establishing a clear and fit -for-purpose feedback mechanism within the M&E framework 
will help capture lessons learned and disseminate them effectively. This will promote 
adaptive management, enabling the project to adjust strategies as needed and enhance 
replicability in other regions and sectors.  
51. Based on the analyses presented in the above paragraphs , the iTAP recommends that 
the Board approve this funding proposal.

CI notes  iTAP’s  positive  assessment  with  thanks.  
CI acknowledges that further work can be done during implementation to ensure that the 
project benefits for coastal communities planned under Component 1 reach the most vulnerable  people  within  the target  communities.  The project  design  allows  for this flexibility 
especially through the activities planned under Project Output 1.2 “Improved livelihood 
activities and more economically productive community businesses”.  
Paradigm  shift  potential  
iTAP  evaluation:  Medium  to High  
CI agrees  with  the importance  placed  on the long -term  financial sustainability  of the project 
activities to ensure long -term benefits. This particularly applies to continuing to support 
community efforts to manage mangroves. Activity 2.2.1 is  designed to  address  this need by 
engaging with the private sector to increase the support companies operating in Ecuador 
provide for mangrove conservation and restoration.  
Sustainable  development  potential  
iTAP  evaluation:  High  
CI agrees  with  the need  for gender -focused  capacity -building  and ensuring  equitable access  to 
project benefits. These issues are highlighted in Annex 8, the Gender Assessment and Action Plan. The Plan highlights how activities are designed to ensure that women and men can 
equitably benefit from community -based management and local economic development 
initiatives.  
CI also notes the suggestion to strengthen collaboration between industry and academia. 
Activity 2.1.1, on the promotion of climate -smart aquaculture, includes a sub -activity to 
disseminate  knowledge  gathered  by the project with  partners,  including  in academia. CI will 
examine ways of strengthening this sub -activity during implementation.

mangroves. Public funding for protection of mangroves is a strategic investment that  
provides substantial environmental, social and economic returns. CI agrees that diversified financing of mangrove protection is needed and has built this into the project design. The 
activities of Component 2 seek to 1) integrate mangrove conservation and restoration as 
standard practice in shrimp aquaculture operations,  and 2) attract private -sector investment 
for community managem ent of mangroves.
